Can Anthropic afford to put safety first?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
13:29:45 UTC
22:29:45 KST

Today, I’m talking with Hayden Field, The Verge’s senior AI reporter, about the Anthropic IPO for the third and final episode in our series about AI in real life.
Can Anthropic afford to put safety first?
The Anthropic IPO raises big questions about the future of AI, the company’s safety-first mission, and how we balance idealism with capitalism.
The AI lab behind Claude went public on the Nasdaq last Thursday. The numbers are gigantic, but not really that important for this conversation, except to say that Anthropic has become one of the most valuable companies on the planet.
The real story is what happens now — and whether Anthropic can continue to thread the needle of caring about and investing in AI safety while also being a public company beholden to shareholders. Oh, and it also continuously growing the amount of money it makes so it can keep paying its ever-increasing compute bills, which total roughly $900 billion over the coming years.
All of these ideas sit in a fascinating tension with one another, and so Hayden and I really tried to break down how we think this might play out as Anthropic navigates the pressures of the public markets.
You’ll hear us talk about the company’s unique corporate governance structure, something that is especially relevant in the wake of the ongoing Jensen Huang saga. We also get into why OpenAI seems happy to have let Anthropic go public first, and why Anthropic’s whole pitch to big customers boils down to the same safety branding that now sits in conflict with the profit-oriented part of its business.
Before we get going, do me a favor and subscribe to Decoder on YouTube, where we put out new episodes every Monday and Thursday.
Okay: Hayden Field, The Verge’s senior AI reporter, on the Anthropic IPO and the economics of frontier AI. Here we go.
This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity.
Hayden Field, welcome back to Decoder.
Thanks. Great to be here.
I’m excited to talk to you today because it’s not pure soap opera drama chaos.
I know.
It’s like a normal thing that happened.
It’s for once not personality-based and soap opera. It’s about cold, hard would-be cash.
[Laughs] Well, there is some personality in it because we’re going to talk a lot about Anthropic’s IPO, which has a bunch of big personalities in it, and the way they have structured this company to repel the rapacious pressures of capitalism is, I would say, very eccentric. It reflects the beliefs of a number of eccentric people.
Yep, absolutely. It’s really been interesting digging into the S-1 again and just seeing who was on what committee and which founders have the most control here and how much control the founders actually have, which is more than it may seem.
Let’s start at the start. You and I are talking on IPO day. Anthropic is the first pure AI lab to go public. They priced the IPO at $83 a share and they got all the way up to $111. By the time people are listening to this, who knows what the price will actually be, but it’s been a real victory lap for all the people who are invested in Anthropic. A bunch of law firms have changed their homepages to be like, “Congratulations, guys!”
What do you make of this? Is it just the thing that had to happen or is it a sign that the AI bubble is not going to pop? How should people think about it?
I don’t think it really gives us new information about whether the AI bubble is going to pop or not, because I mean, this is peak AI hype right here. It’s the first AI lab to go public. It’s been kind of on track to break records for months. Everyone was like, “It’s probably going to beat Musk,” and then it did. I can’t wait to see his tweets later.
But the other interesting part of this is that I’ve heard thousands of new millionaires and hundreds of new centimillionaires will be born from this, not to mention billionaires, too. It’s one of those things where I think the AI bubble is continuing to swell.
I was even chatting with some tech workers inside a bunch of different companies this morning, and they were all saying within their companies, they’re seeing the bubble swell and swell, and they’re being tasked with doing more and more AI, even if it makes them less productive and makes the work less good.
So I think there’s going to be a reckoning eventually, but right now we’re at peak AI hype and Anthropic does a great job of marketing its business as safe AI. Of course, they’re going to have more pressure around that marketing term now more than ever. So it’s going to be interesting to see the tension point kind of go into the spotlight and see how they handle this.
Verge subscribers, don’t forget you get exclusive access to ad-free Decoder wherever you get your podcasts. Head here. Not a subscriber? You can sign up here.
I was at the Semafor conference earlier this week. I saw Sam Altman and Sarah Friar, OpenAI’s CFO, on stage there. Altman and Greg Brockman were at the DealBook conference this week. I get the sense they’re kind of happy that Anthropic went first to see what happens in the markets and to see how companies value the revenue potential of their big competitors so they can tell their IPO story better.
Is that the sense you get, that Anthropic is the trial balloon? Or is OpenAI just retconning the fact that it didn’t go first?
Yes, I think that’s absolutely the case. I heard there was a time in the past year that Altman was really obsessed with beating Anthropic to go public. That’s what I heard. It was all he thought about. But then with all the bad press around OpenAI this year, they’ve had setback after setback. A lot of the public is particularly focused on hating OpenAI right now.
Obviously, there’s a big AI populism backlash and a lot of people hate a lot of AI, but it seems like OpenAI has really borne the brunt of a lot of public hatred and scandals this year. And so they were like, “You know what? Let’s just take a step back and let Anthropic go first.” And they seem happy to do that.
Now that Anthropic has gone first and set this historic precedent, OpenAI can ride on its coattails and say, “Yeah, us too.” I doubt they would beat Anthropic’s record. Maybe they will, maybe they won’t. But either way, I think they know that things could have gone a lot worse for them if Anthropic did not go first, if it’s happening this year.
I can’t stop thinking about Anthropic in terms of just dumb business fundamentals now that it’s a public company. You buy a token from them. They have to make that token for less than you pay for it. They also want to make AGI. Somewhere else they have some other huge set of costs.
Just based on what we know about the business today, now that it’s a public company, are they profitable on the face of it? Do they have a long way to go before inference is so profitable it can pay for training all the new models? How does their business actually work?
They have a long way to go and that is why it’s going to be so interesting to see what exactly they do here with regard to driving more revenue, which is what they’re going to have to do. I saw one estimate from Steve Rattner saying Anthropic is going to have to increase its revenue roughly 17-fold within the next 10 years to deliver a comparable return to some of its competitors in the tech industry.
But even without that specific number, you can tell. I mean, when a company goes public, investors want to see a return on their investment. They want to see profits go up or just profits at all. They also want to see that even if profits aren’t a thing yet, they want to see that you’re driving so much revenue that they could see profit happening in the near term. That is something that Anthropic is going to have to reckon with more than ever before after today.
I was chatting with a bunch of corporate governance experts and law professors and they were all saying Anthropic, like OpenAI, has such an unusual corporate structure that they aren’t actually beholden to shareholders in the way that some public companies are. Shareholders can never vote out executives. They don’t have a lot of power to influence anything really. It’s the founders and this special trust, which we can get to in a second, that have the power.
But either way, they were all saying it doesn’t matter if shareholders have a say here really in voting. It’s about the fact that no matter who has a say, Anthropic is going to be under so much pressure to make money and to buy compute and catch up with OpenAI in that way, that this is going to be a huge reckoning for them. We’re probably going to see a lot of things fall by the wayside, maybe even some expensive safety efforts.
I want to come to the governance structure angle here. We live in a time of Nvidia scandals. Corporate governance is all the rage now. It’s the hottest topic in American business, but let’s come to that in one minute.
I just want to stay on “can you sell a token for a dollar more than it cost you to make it” for one more turn. It’s just the dumbest way of thinking about this. Anthropic rents capacity from big cloud providers, from hyperscalers. They run the GPUs in them. They pay for power, they pay for the GPUs. There’s some input cost and then they have to sell the token for more than all those input costs.
That is the dumbest way of thinking about their business. I promise you that they think about it in a more sophisticated way than that, but that is how I think about it. I was just at the Microsoft Ignite conference this week and I saw Satya Nadella and he was very clear to me and others that they’re going to try to bring the price of tokens down, that their whole system is designed to make all the AI vendors fungible underneath Copilot and they’ll pick the best model for the task and make sure you get the cheapest one for whatever it is you’re trying to do.
That seems totally at odds with what Anthropic needs to do, which is get more and more margin for every single token they sell so they can keep renting capacity from data centers or buy more chips than OpenAI or whatever it is they need to do. How are they structured to solve that problem? Is it just more people using AI or is it more expensive tokens?
I think it’s a little of both, and it’s interesting because Anthropic has been kind of sneakily trying to do this. We wrote a piece in the past year about how there was a big outcry, particularly focused on Anthropic, for passing on costs to consumers. Individuals who were customers of Anthropic were really mad because they felt like the prices were going up for the same stuff or for worse quality and that costs were just getting passed on to them.
This became a thing across the industry, a big outcry, but Anthropic bore the brunt of it. Anthropic had to have a public statement. They’re like, “Look, we’re going to work on this.” And they did roll it back a little, but I mean, they’ve got to keep doing it in some way.
The other thing they’re focused on is hoping that their models are going to be good enough that people just form an attachment — not emotionally, well maybe — but form an attachment to them and they can moat people in. They’re all about the moat these days: “We’ve got to build a moat.” OpenAI is about this, too. They want so much of your everyday life and business to be married to Anthropic’s tools that it’d be really hard to port it all over and you won’t want to and you’ll just keep paying. So that’s what they’re going for.
They’re also, of course, hoping that enterprise and government contracts are going to really make up a bunch of this, in which case you don’t have to worry quite as much about nickel-and-diming because they’ve got a lot of money. But I think that’s the interesting part here. When it comes to people that do care about the price a lot, they’re just trying to moat them in and hope for the best.
What’s interesting there is that consumers are sensitive to price in one way. I think Anthropic’s big business customers, which is where they’ve been historically focused, are sensitive to price in different ways.
If you’re paying Anthropic a lot of money, you might want to be able to switch providers quickly and you might demand data sovereignty and all this stuff that big enterprises are thinking about. Anthropic either has to deliver that, which would kill their moat for their big enterprise customers, or say, “We’re so good, you can’t have that.” I’m not sure that they can say the second one yet.
That’s why they’re marketing this safe, trustworthy AI line so deeply, because it’s the only thing they’ve really got going for them here. When you have an enterprise customer, most of the time you do have to give them data sovereignty. You can’t trade on their data. There’s a lot of stuff that Anthropic could be doing to make more money that they can’t do when someone is paying that much.
I think that’s really why they’re driving home this point of being the safe, trustworthy alternative because that’s the only thing that’s really going to get people to stick around, particularly if they also drive home the cybersecurity tools in an age when everything’s getting hacked all the time.
The other interesting thing is even not with huge enterprises, but with small to medium-sized businesses, I’ve been chatting with a bunch of them and they’ve all been creating their own internal tools to kind of track which tasks they need specifically to use an expensive Anthropic model for and which ones they can port over to maybe an open-source model or a really cheap model.
So a lot of companies like law firms — and people in a lot of different industries that have small to medium-sized businesses — are literally creating their own internal benchmarks about, “Okay, let’s save money as much as we can to just only put 10 percent of our tasks toward the expensive Anthropic model, and then this 40 percent can go to an open-source model and this 50 percent can go to a mid-tier, cheaper model.”
So that’s what’s really interesting to me. People are trying to figure this out on their own and not even sharing those benchmarks publicly because it’s their own way of budgeting.
I actually heard Nadella this week say every company will have its own set of evaluations and they will test the models against them and always use the most efficient one and this will become one of the major things that every company does.
The flip side to that is I’ve heard Anthropic executives say, “Well, you will always want the frontier model because your competitor will always be using the frontier model and if you use a cheaper one, you’ll be left behind.” And that has always seemed somewhere between naive and idealistic to me because I think a lot of companies do cheap things knowing that their competitors will also do cheap things.
I agree completely. Everyone’s being cheap.
Yeah, I think if you can get away with good enough, you’re going to find out exactly where good enough is and you will not assume that your competitor is going to eke out a 5 percent advantage by using a more expensive model. Somewhere on the margin is where all of the costs should be and the market will figure it out.
Exactly.
It just feels like the basics of the business, maybe on IPO day, the pressure isn’t on that, everyone’s just very happy and rich. The next quarter’s earnings are going to be where we start to see this shakeout. Can it keep growing the way it’s growing at the margins it needs to build everything, especially when its own suppliers are now major shareholders?
Totally. And we saw one quarter where they experienced huge growth and then the next quarter wasn’t that much. So yeah, I mean they’re going to have a lot to reckon with.
Let’s talk about the governance issues. Because of Nvidia, corporate governance is the hottest topic in American business. Like you said, OpenAI and Anthropic have some of the most complicated governance structures in history.
If you think that Anthropic’s future is to sell the best intelligence — the most capable, the most cutting-edge frontier, close-to-AGI intelligence possible — you would probably not do a bunch of safety work. You would probably not kill your margins on a bunch of researchers who are telling you to stop or slow down or not release the best model.
Anthropic is deeply committed to doing that. You and I have had conversations before about whether that can withstand the pressures of capitalism and growth. That was hard when they weren’t a public company, but now they are. Talk about Anthropic’s structure and how they’re trying to codify their commitment to safety.
Yeah, that’s a really great question. I just published a piece about this. It’s so interesting to me that Anthropic, despite seeing all the roller-coaster drama that OpenAI went through around the Sam Altman ouster and all of the restructurings that that company has gone through, Anthropic has a similarly unusual structure. And in a way it’s helpful because they’re not beholden, as we said, to shareholder pressure besides if shareholders start selling off their stock or don’t buy more.
But the two kinds of groups of people that have power over what happens at Anthropic are the founders and this group called the Long-Term Benefit Trust (LTBT). Anthropic likes to market this as being like, “Oh, don’t worry about that the founders are doing what’s best for humanity but with no checks. We’ve got this trust above us that can appoint or remove us anytime.” As we’ll see in the movie Artificial, Sam Altman famously says, “Oh, well, the board can fire me whenever.” He says that a million times.
Now, Anthropic is implying the same thing: “Oh, we have this trust above us. They can remove or appoint the majority of board members,” even though it’s actually not all of them, but we’ll get to that in a minute. So this trust is a group of right now four people and none of them have a lot of AI experience. It’s [former Federal Reserve Chairman] Ben Bernanke, a foreign policy analyst named Richard Fontaine, an experimental physicist named Christopher Stubbs, and a former GiveWell executive named Neil Buddy Shah, who is chair of the LTBT.
I spoke to a couple of former Anthropic employees that left not too long ago and one of them said that this trust is kind of handpicked to not rock the boat at Anthropic. The founders appointed them. They’re not going to be appointing someone that’s going to probably disagree with them a lot.
Ironically, the trust doesn’t have the authority to step in on everyday decisions at Anthropic, unlike even the OpenAI nonprofit board that existed. They could say, “Oh look, don’t release this model. It’s too dangerous.” The trust doesn’t have any of those powers. All it can do is appoint or remove certain board members. Even then, they have to give 30 days’ notice to a small committee of the board, which includes Daniela Amodei, the chair of that little committee who is also a founder of Anthropic.
To oversimplify things, out of these seven board seats, the trust gets to pick three, the founders get to pick three, and then the kind of tie-breaking seat, the seventh one, the trust technically appoints, but they have to basically get founder approval to do it. So it’s one of these things that’s so complex, but when you really look at the nitty-gritty of it, the founders have pretty unchecked control here. I think that’s something that’s getting lost in the narrative of, “Oh, this trust is above us. Don’t worry.” Okay, well, they don’t really have much power to check you.
The founder control structure is the wildest thing I have ever read about in years of doing the show and thinking about business. Anthropic has seven founders. They have been issued a single share of stock that holds 50.1 percent of the voting power of the company. That is issued to a company called Founder LLC, and they’re the seven co-owners of Founder LLC.
How on earth does that work? Do they just all get together and then decide how to vote the one share of stock?
Yeah, it’s really interesting and that’s why I was kind of oversimplifying it a minute ago with who gets to choose this tie-breaking seat because it’s technically Founder LLC. It’s just a total mess. But when you really look at the simplified version, it’s that yes, through all of these weird structures and things they’ve done, they basically have pretty unchecked power and that’s the way they want it.
When I was talking to a former employee who recently left, they said it’s not necessarily that the founders have a financial interest in making the most money. In their eyes, it’s more that they have an interest in unchecked control. I think that’s a really important point here.
Some dissenters within the ranks of Anthropic think it’s not about them wanting to get rich, but rather it’s about them wanting that total control, which does track with a lot of their public statements. They say, “You can’t do safety from second place.” They’re all in on speeding up this race, but the problem is when you speed up the AI race, you’re speeding it up for everyone involved.
The safety piece is the really interesting portion of this to me because it’s the thing you would spend money on, absent some huge litigation drama or liability concern, that no other company would spend money on.
So let’s say it’s proven in court that model companies are liable for what people’s agents do. I think then all the model companies would build safety systems. They would have some big incentive to make sure their models are safe. That has not yet happened. We don’t know. There’s a big debate about safety and where the regulation will come from and what will create the incentives.
In the meantime, you’ve got seven founders holding a single share of stock who right now appear to be aligned on investing in safety in various ways inside of Anthropic. What happens if they start to disagree?
Yeah, that’s a great question. I think that the other interesting question here is what happens when you can explain away anything in the name of safety if you’re thinking it’s “for the greater good,” which as we know is a common villain trope in every movie. But it’s also one of Anthropic’s main things on its website. They’re like, “We want to do this for the greater good, for the global good.”
If you’re saying, “Hey, we have to win this race at all costs because we’re the only good guys and anyone else who wins it is going to do worse than us,” are you really the good guy at that point? And can’t you just explain away any single decision, even dropping some safety measures, as saying, “Oh, it’s slowing us down too much. We’re going to lose. We’ve got to win at all costs”? So you drop this or drop that. And then you look in the mirror at the end of the race and you say, “Oh, actually, we’re the exact same as the other company we’re trying to be different from.” I think that’s increasingly happening.
So I mean, right now they’ve said, “Look, we’re really committed to this.” You do have to remember that these people are almost religiously dedicated to this idea of safe AI. A lot of them think that AI systems are potentially conscious. I mean, this is by Anthropic founders’ own statements.
So I think that it’s not like they’re just secretly only trying to make money and just talking about safety and they don’t mean it. I think it’s more something a little more dangerous in that they want complete control because they believe that they have the right plan for AI safety and they will do it better than anyone else.
But as we know, absolute power corrupts absolutely. So a lot of Anthropic employees that I was talking to, former ones, were saying, “Yeah, it does not make sense that this industry is so unregulated. There’s no external regulation. Even if these seven people think they have the best idea for AI safety, okay, then prove it to someone else — that you shouldn’t be the only ones deciding this. Because you’re not elected.” So that’s the consensus among some.
They have a morally binding handshake agreement to allow external auditors, Hayden. It’s going to be fine.
Oh, yeah, with employee-level access.
Also, it’s called “Super Intelligence” now. It’s going to be totally fine. They all went to the White House and they looked deeply into each other’s eyes and said it was going to be fine.
Obviously Anthropic came up with this structure well before the events with Nvidia and Jensen Huang, but I can’t help but put them together in my head. They are happening at the same time. And Nvidia has a very traditional corporate governance structure.
Jensen owned 3 percent of the company, but he had a board of directors. The board of directors determined that he had violated the company’s code of conduct in some way. He resigned. It’s very obvious that he was thoroughly encouraged to resign, but that’s just normal corporate governance. That’s what you learn about in school. A company has a board of directors. Their number one job is to hire and fire the CEO.
That’s very different in Silicon Valley, right? Founder control of companies is a big story everywhere. Mark Zuckerberg famously has control over Meta, even though Meta shareholders repeatedly vote to remove him, which is one of the underemphasized stories in all of American business. Sergey Brin and Larry Page control Google.
Is this more in the, “We need to insulate our founders from the demands of the market and boards of directors” in classic Silicon Valley style? Or is it, “We need to insulate our founders and Dario from the demands of the market so we can stay focused on AGI and safety”? Because it feels like a combination of both.
It’s definitely a combination. I mean, they say it’s the latter. I think in a lot of ways it’s like the former. It’s totally a combination. I’d say it’s about 50/50. I mean, they want control so that they can focus on what they think is best and not lose control to someone that they don’t think knows best. But ironically, as we know, no one group of people should be in control of what is best here. So at least that’s what a lot of my sources say.
So I think that, yeah, I mean, it’s just a combination. Elon Musk famously won’t run a company that he doesn’t have voting control over. So I think it’s just a new Valley thing and it’s pervading here for a couple of different reasons.
There are seven of them. What seven different people do over the course of the next five to 10 years is as unpredictable as anything. What happens if one of them does something illegal or lies to the board or smuggles chips to China or anything? Is there some way to remove one of these founders from this LLC?
Yeah. I mean, allegedly the trust has the power to remove. They haven’t said whether they have the power to remove all of the board or just some, but in some way when you’re looking at the fine print of the S-1, basically if the trust gives 30 days’ notice to essentially Daniela Amodei and her other two people on this subcommittee, then they can suggest removal of a board member. Again, they can waive that 30-day notice if they deem it correct, if Daniela does.
But yeah, there’s a lot of fine print here. Essentially, yes, you can remove someone and there is stuff about that in the S-1 saying if they’ve done something illegal, if they’ve done something really bad, they can be removed, but it’s definitely not easy. It also depends on who it is, I think.
It’s interesting. I keep coming back to this because it is so complicated, it’s so unusual, and it is right next to the Nvidia situation where he materially misrepresented his relationship to the shareholders by having his PR people issue statements saying it was all normal. He had to admit that it wasn’t all normal and he was in fact involved in extramarital affairs and the board fired him.
I felt a little bad for that PR guy who had to put his name on all those statements that ended up being a lie.
I feel nothing. As the audience may know, as Hayden is well aware, we make all the PR people put their names on everything just in case something like this happens. That’s just a little bit of Verge trivia.
Anyhow, right, I mean, that’s just normal governance. Here you have a company where the only check might be the market itself, right?
Yep.
If they can’t sell tokens for more than what they make them for, they’re going to have to report that in the financials and the market might say this company is failing and pull all the money back. That is a very different kind of pressure.
If the only meaningful way that Dario might lose his job is if the company goes bankrupt and then the swirl of things is he’s got to buy chips before OpenAI can buy the chips, he’s got to increase the number of verticals that Anthropic competes in within the enterprise, and he also idealistically has to train the next model and the next model with RSI to get to AGI, it feels like actually the pressure is to go as fast as possible. If I just added that up, I would say I would not slow down ever.
Yeah. That’s why I say you can kind of explain away anything if you’re trying to be first, which they are. And they’ve been very public about that. Regarding the money thing, Ann Lipton, a law professor at the University of Colorado Law School, told me this week that the trust and the founders together are the ones who actually have governance power and that you either trust that they’re going to balance the interest of society appropriately or you don’t.
But either way, the real constraint on whatever impulses they have is going to be the fact that Anthropic needs money. That’s the thing, Anthropic needs money. In a livestream recently, Dario told the CEO of JPMorgan Chase that compute is the “deepest business dilemma” that the company faces. Essentially if you buy too much, you can financially overextend yourself. If you buy too little, then you can’t support inference for customers.
Even more importantly, you can’t do the R&D. You can’t come up with new models. So they’re extremely compute-constrained, even maybe more so than OpenAI because OpenAI was made fun of for a long time for losing a ton of money, but they were investing a lot in compute. Anthropic did not do that as much, and now they’re having to pay the piper.
So it’s an interesting turnaround here. Hopefully for them and their interests, I guess, if they can get the compute they need. But as we know, the supply is a lot lower than the demand. So it’s just going to be interesting to see what they will do to make money and therefore money to buy compute.
It’s funny. A couple of weeks ago I told our colleague, Liz Lopatto, that the Anthropic IPO would be one of the biggest stories of the year. She was laughing at me today, because she says it’s not even the biggest story of the week because of Jensen Huang and Nvidia.
My argument — take it or leave it, and you can judge whether I’m correct — is that they are the same story. That if Nvidia’s financing architecture fails or wobbles because Jensen isn’t there to bully everyone into believing in it, then maybe you do have a glut of chips sitting in data centers without the appropriate permitting or power to light them up and the market for chips will change dramatically.
Maybe a company like CoreWeave does get way more wobbly or some of the already wobbly neoclouds start to get wobbly and the price of compute falls and lets Anthropic compete differently. Maybe a bunch of chips that were allocated to China on the gray market come flooding back to the United States.
That apparently will happen.
Right? So there’s a lot of ways the Nvidia story affects Anthropic’s financials. Has the company said anything about that yet? Is the market reacting to that in any way?
Not yet. They’ve just talked about how important compute is and that it’s going to be a really big issue for them. And Anthropic’s IPO, it has a lot of ties to Nvidia and there’s a lot of complexities here because Nvidia had agreed to buy $10 billion in Anthropic shares via private placement on top of a $10 billion funding promise it made last year.
So they are in bed together, and that’s going to be interesting to see, too. The new CEO of Nvidia has made a lot of claims about diverting chips that were potentially going to China and bringing them back so they’re not doing that anymore.
Now we’re going to see if there’s more supply or not. Also, Jensen personally made some of these deals, especially with large financial institutions to fund the AI infrastructure buildout last August. So he’s wheeling and dealing a lot. And so it’s going to remain to be seen whether the new chief executive has that kind of power to wheel and deal and pressure people into doing stuff like this or not.
This is my last question. Obviously everyone’s just celebrating today. We are probably throwing the most cold water on this by noting that the structure of Anthropic is very odd. What should people be looking for? What are the signs that you’re looking for now that Anthropic is a stock that’s trading? What’s the relationship in your mind between the value of Anthropic stock on any given day and the broader AI story?
I think that stock is a little bit like benchmarks in the AI industry in that sometimes it doesn’t mean what you think it means and also it never means everything. So you always take it with a grain of salt. It’s all vibes based. It’s all hype. So people get scared, they sell, and people hear one good thing or a rumor and they buy. So I think it’s important to look at it and see what’s driving that fear or hype that day or that week. But it’s also really important to see what the company says in these reports and earnings calls.
That’s what I think is the most interesting. Not really the numbers all the time, but instead, what they try to sneak into these reports by saying, “Oh, we sidelined this team,” or, “We discontinued this safety initiative all in the name of being first.”
That’s the type of thing that I think we’re going to see a lot of and that’s what I want to watch for. Because the company that made its bed on safe AI is increasingly looking the same as OpenAI and is going to have a lot of swept-under-the-rug type of updates like that, if my sources are right.
You heard it here first. Former CNBC reporter Hayden Field says, look past the day-to-day swings into the fundamentals of the company. That’s good. Also, the stock market is only vibes. That’s the other thing I heard you say.
Yep. I mean, it’s controversial, but after being a financial reporter, yeah, I mean, there’s a lot of vibes there. It’s a lot of vibes.
Oh, I agree. I think sometimes the only thing they ask me to go on CNBC to say is this is just vibes, and then they usher me off the air very quickly.
Hayden, this is great. We’re obviously going to be talking about this story many times with you in the year to come. Hopefully we can do another straightforward business story and not just a soap opera one.
I’ll be here for both. We’ve got to do them both.
Amazing. Thank you so much, Hayden.
Thanks.

Astra 6.2 and Sol 6.2 flop at Simple Bench
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
13:16:12 UTC
22:16:12 KST

We introduce SimpleBench, a multiple-choice text benchmark for LLMs where individuals
                            with
                            unspecialized (high school) knowledge outperform SOTA models. SimpleBench includes over 200
                            questions covering spatio-temporal reasoning, social intelligence, and what we call
                            linguistic adversarial robustness (or trick questions). For the vast majority of text-based
                            benchmarks LLMs outperform a non-specialized human, and increasingly, exceed expert human
                            performance. However, on SimpleBench, a non-specialized human baseline is 83.7%, based on
                            our small sample of nine participants, outperforming every tested LLM, including
                            today's top model, Claude Fable, which scored 81.9%. While we expect model performance to improve over time, the
                            results of SimpleBench confirm that the memorized knowledge, and approximate reasoning
                            retrieval, utilized by frontier LLMs is not always enough to answer basic questions just
                            yet. 
For a deeper dive into our results and our methods, check out the full technical report here.
Use all of these models on the Simple Bench app - LMcouncil.ai

Mercury-Bound X-Ray Telescope Delivers First Images After Eight Years In Space
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
11:00:00 UTC
20:00:00 KST

Mercury-Bound X-Ray Telescope Delivers First Images After Eight Years In Space (phys.org) 6
			
		 	
				A British-built X-ray telescope aboard the European-Japanese BepiColombo spacecraft has captured its first astronomical images after eight years in space and roughly 6.2 billion miles of travel. Phys.org reports:  A team led by the University of Leicester has been making calibration measurements with the Mercury Imaging X-ray Spectrometer (MIXS), one of the instruments on the two-spacecraft BepiColombo mission to Mercury, which begins its orbital insertion tomorrow (Nov. 21) in preparation for the final stage of arrival at our solar system's smallest planet. After an eight-year journey, it is vital that the team checks how MIXS is performing. Although they have been monitoring the instrument during its trip, it was only in September, following the separation of the Mercury Transfer Module (MTM) from the joined science orbiters, Mercury Planetary Orbiter (MPO) and Mercury Magnetospheric Orbiter (MMO) or Mio, that MIXS could finally see into space for the first time and calibration measurements could be made before the orbit insertion sequence begins.
The Crab Nebula is 6,500 light-years away and a well-characterized astrophysical source of X-ray light. By observing it, the team can work out the performance characteristics of MIXS in space and compare these with calibration measurements made with the same instrument in the laboratory in 2015. These observations provide MIXS's first view of an astronomical X-ray source. The main observation sequence consisted of 55 spacecraft slews, with 39 hours of observations during Sept. 11-12. Measuring how much the Crab Nebula's image is spread across MIXS's detector helps the scientists measure MIXS's angular resolution, or how sharply it will be able to distinguish features on Mercury's surface. They found this to be slightly better than when it was last measured in the lab in 2015. 
"During our calibration observations we have determined that MIXS is generally performing fantastically well in space! We have no effects in our images that are associated with 'stray light' (optical photons getting in when they should not). We continue to see effects of galactic cosmic rays as we did during cruise, but we understand these and can filter them out," said Professor Emma Bunch, Principal Investigator (PI) for the MIXS instrument and Director of the Institute for Space at the University of Leicester.
"We see some self-fluorescence which appears when the sun is illuminating the optic, but this won't affect our dayside observations of Mercury's surface. And the detector has suffered no radiation damage after eight years in space. We see some effects in the instrument that we are still studying and expect to undertake some optimization procedures during the Mercury commissioning phase to fine-tune MIXS in preparation for the science phase."

====================================================================================================

My Monitor Was Cutting My Download Speed by 75%
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
10:36:32 UTC
19:36:32 KST

My Monitor Was Cutting My Download Speed by 75%
Somehow I am one of those few very lucky people to have the opportunity of owning a 2.5 gig symmetrical line for my internet. But one day, when I wanted to brag to my equally nerdy friends, shock and horror: 600 Mbps down? Luckily my upload speed was still 2.4 Gbps, so I didn’t lose all my bragging rights, but that’s weird, right? If anything, you’d expect the opposite!
My ISP is pretty cool. It’s run by this one guy (Hey Keith!), and I know I can always message them and they respond promptly, as they did this time. We tried to run through a few debugging steps, they checked on their end, and everything was normal. I tried my work laptop too, same speed.
I didn’t have time for debugging, because I needed to travel 2000 km to the other side of the continent for a family emergency.
But as soon as I got back, I got curious again. What could it be? In retrospect, I now know when it started: when I moved all my connections onto my Studio Display XDR. I wanted to have a one-cable setup, and the monitor offered me that. I’ve had it for half a year now, and I don’t think I’ve ever run a speed test through it. 600 Mbps was more than fast enough for me to never realise I wasn’t getting three quarters of the speed I was paying for 🤦
The setup#
- MacBook Pro M3 Pro
- 2026 Studio Display XDR, with everything plugged into it so switching laptops is one cable
- UGREEN USB-C to 2.5G Ethernet adapter, plugged into the display
- UniFi Dream Router 7, the same one from my WiFi 7 debugging post
What I thought was interesting#
Pings to other wired computers during a download would fail 10% of the time over Ethernet through the adapter plugged into my screen, and 0% of the time over Wi-Fi. What made it stranger was that there were no dropped frame errors in macOS’s logs at all. This was the big hint for me: the adapter was dropping them before the computer could know.
I then plugged the adapter straight into my Mac instead of the monitor, and ran an iperf3 server on my UDR 7: 2.3 Gbps. So the cable, the router and the adapter are all fine? What about fast.com? Perfect speeds there too! 2.2 down and 2.3 up when connected straight in. As soon as I connected it to the display, it was back to 600 down again.
So it’s the display, right? Well, I would find out: sort of yes, sort of no, which is why I wanted to write this blog post. I couldn’t find anything online explaining the exact cause, and it’s quite a cool reason.
The driver and flow control#
The adapter uses a Realtek RTL8156B, which on macOS binds to Apple’s generic USB networking driver. But Realtek ship their own driver on their website, so I thought I’d give that a go! I installed it and, to my surprise, it almost doubled the download speed to 1.1 Gbps, but that was still nowhere close to 2.5. First hint that it couldn’t just be the monitor’s fault.
Then I tried flow control. I had never truly looked into this before, and I think it’s quite neat to know about if you were also ignorant of it like me. Basically, when a device receives Ethernet frames it can’t keep up with, it just drops them. With flow control (it’s something your router or switch has to support), it can send the sender a “pause” frame, and the sender then buffers the data on its side, instead of the receiver (the adapter in this case) on its side. I turned it on in my UniFi console and guess what, download jumped to around 2 Gbps, with upload still at full speed! Yay! Well, not so fast…
I don’t like it always being on, because it can add latency to other systems like my gaming PC. Can you work around it? For sure, you can disable it on those computers, but it’s janky, and it’s another thing I need to remember when my Sauerbraten game starts lagging. I need fewer things to remember, considering I am 30 in a couple of months. My memory is not what it used to be.
But the upload was always fine#
Yeah, isn’t that weird? First of all, it looks like the port can indeed do 2.5 in both directions. I already knew that, because I get much more than that from my external SSD through the monitor’s Thunderbolt port. So the issue is not the monitor again? But it has to be!
Well, I had another idea. How about I connect to my Linux box, which is also on 2.5, and send perfectly smooth UDP traffic at a fixed rate with flow control off? Then I can check the chip’s own counter of dropped frames:
So basically no drops whatsoever when connected straight to my Mac, but loads of drops after about 168k frames per second (roughly 2 Gbps). Remember that number. That number comes from the adapter’s own counters: however much I sent, the number of frames it managed to hand over to the Mac each second flattened out at about 168,000, and everything above that was dropped.
Why did this not happen on my SSD? What was the only difference I could find? Bursts!
When the Mac reads data from a USB 3 device, the device sends some packets, then has to wait for the Mac to say “lgtm, send more!”. The SSD enclosure sends 16 packets before needing a go-ahead message back. The RTL8156B in my Ethernet adapter sends 4.
Here is a diagram of my best understanding of why this is an issue. I made this diagram 100 times, and I think I can probably improve it more, but if it helps you understand the issue, I have done my job.
As you can see, the speed of packets coming into the adapter is 2.5 Gbps. That’s what the router pushes in. The speed going out is around 2 Gbps. The reason is that if you add up the average time it takes to get the go-ahead through my monitor’s hub, which is around 7.6 µs, to the average time it takes to send 4 KB, around 8.2 µs, you can find what the maximum speed is.
Well, if the speed of the drain from my adapter to my Mac is half a gig slower than the speed of the input, then you can imagine the buffer getting filled more and more until it overflows. Those are dropped packets, which result in a hard backoff when the router resends them, so you drop loads in speed. And that’s the reason for all of this.
OK, well, what now?#
I have a cupboard full of USB-C Ethernet adapters. Don’t ask.
The problem is they’re all 1 gig adapters, so any of the tests I made would not tell me anything, because the issues don’t start at 1 gig. They start at around 2… If I run my 2.5 gig adapter at 1 gig speeds, I get 1 gig downloads with the official driver. If I run it at 1.5, I get 1.5. If I run it at 2, well, I get 1…
So I thought I would do what any human would do, and I spent more money on a new adapter. Another UGREEN, but this time it’s one that promises 5 Gbps and uses the RTL8157 chip instead: UGREEN 5G USB-C Ethernet adapter. My reasoning was that if this one could get me better speeds, it would help me understand how. And that it did!
With this adapter, I dropped 0 frames:
Why? Well, looking through what each adapter tells me about itself:
$ python3 -c "import usb.core; e = usb.core.find(idVendor=0x0bda)[0][(0,0)][0]; print('packet size', e.wMaxPacketSize, 'bMaxBurst', e.extra_descriptors[2])"
# old adapter (RTL8156B)
packet size 1024 bMaxBurst 3
# new adapter (RTL8157)
packet size 1024 bMaxBurst 7
# bMaxBurst counts the packets after the first one, so 3 means 4 packets and 7 means 8
I can see that instead of sending 4 1 KB packets per burst, it would send 8! And the speed of the link was also 2x faster, so those 8 KB would go 2x faster to the Mac. That means the time spent was 8 times lower to send the same data, and while the wait for the go-ahead was identical, the theoretical speed from the adapter to the Mac, including the wait for the go-ahead, was a maximum of 4.5 Gbps. That’s much bigger than the 2.5 coming into the adapter, so the buffer would never fill up, packets would never drop and everything was great, even if the “go-ahead” response was still much slower than when connected directly to the Mac.
Final thoughts#
I think the big takeaway from this is that all the things you think are quite easy to understand, such as transfer speeds, have so many cool intricacies that it’s worth diving deep every so often. Who would’ve thought that the speed of the link between the adapter and the Mac was defined not only by the link speed itself in Gbps, but also by the time it took your Mac to reply, by the amount of packets, by the speed of those packets, by the number of packets in a burst, and by the size of those packets?
See you next time, when I will spend just as much time on another trivial thing that we can all learn from!

====================================================================================================

FBI reportedly investigating apparent hack of its criminal database
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
10:33:57 UTC
19:33:57 KST

FBI reportedly investigating apparent hack of its criminal database
The FBI is investigating an apparent breach of its criminal information database, US media reports, citing two people briefed on the issue.
The National Crime Information Center (NCIC) database is used by federal and local law enforcement to share information on warrants and background checks.
It would not be the first time hackers have targeted the FBI, the domestic intelligence and security service of the US. The agency did not respond to the BBC's request for comment.
When asked about the breach in a Fox News interview on Saturday, President Donald Trump said he had "just heard" about the hack. "I will say that it's not good. I don't like that," he said.
"But maybe we'll learn things that we would've never learned otherwise," Trump added.
This apparent breach of FBI database comes after a string of tech outages which hit banking, air traffic control and other sectors in the US last week. Officials have said they were not caused by cyber-attacks, but the blackouts have sparked scrutiny from lawmakers.
White House National Cyber Director Sean Cairncross was fired this week, with Trump saying he was not good enough.
Dan Lorhmann, a former analyst for the National Security Agency (NSA), told the BBC News Channel that the NCIC's data included "critical information relating to nationwide criminal activity" and needed to be protected.
He added that while US security agencies including the FBI have "an amazing ability" to keep such information safe" and are "globally respected", the level of cyber-attacks is rising globally.
"There's a lot of different reasons why that's happening but I do have faith in our experts in government," Lorhmann said.
In September, the FBI said it was investigating an apparent breach by a cyber-crime group ShinyHunters, which claimed it had stolen sensitive information on all bureau staff - around 38,000 people.
After that hack, one former FBI agent told the BBC he feared for the safety of current employees, whose personal information could soon become available online to criminals and hostile nation-state hackers.
In a separate development, NBC reported an apparent cyberattack against the US Department of Agriculture (USDA).
Employees were informed on Thursday that its farm payment system had been targeted, NBC said.
The USDA has not publicly commented on the issue.

RCE in Gnupg
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
08:32:58 UTC
17:32:58 KST

[Announce] GnuPG Security Advisory for GPGME (T8557,CVE-2026-129975)
Werner Koch 
    wk at gnupg.org
       
Fri Nov 20 19:01:51 CET 2026
    
    
Hello!
We are pleased to announce the availability of a new GPGME release:
version 2.3.1.  This version fixes a *critical security bug* found in
all prior versions.  We also release a new Gpg4win versions and and
GnuPG Desktop and GnuPG VS-Desktop versions which all make use of GPGME.
Description
===========
By using an OpenPGP key containing a crafted very long user id it is
possible to trigger a double free.  This double free can be used
exfiltrate data from applications by sending for example signed data.
Exploiting this bug to mount an Remote Code Execution attack (RCE) can't
be ruled out.  Thus an immediate update is highly suggested.
Our internal ID is T8557 (https://static.dev.gnupg.org/T8557)
The CVE-id is CVE-2026-129975.
This vulnerability was discovered by Johan Carlsson and we were informed
by GitLab on 2026-11-12.  Initially we set 2026-11-25 for a coordinated
disclosure; due to the new Ruby2Shell bug GitLab had to update earlier
and thus we also disclosed the GPGME bug on 2026-11-20.
The root cause for this bug are two GPGME internal functions to read the
status lines and key listings from gpg.  Initially a buffer of 1024
bytes is allocated which is reallocated if more space is required.  When
reallocating the pointer to the new buffer is used by the function but
it is only assigned back to the context object at the end of the
function.  Thus if the function returns early due to an read error or
other events the context is not updated and keeps the stale pointer to
the freed memory.  Eventually the context is released and the memory
freed a second time.  Due to other allocations in between the common
double free checks of memory allocators may not be triggered.
Impact
======
All software using a GPGME version prior to 2.3.0 either as shared
libary, DLL, or statically linked is affected.  Examples of software
using GPGME are
 - Gpg4win
 - GnuPG Desktop
 - GnuPG VS-Desktop
 - Most mail clients
 - Many XMPP clients
 - Samba
 - Flatpack
Solution
========
Please update your GPGME shared library to version 2.3.1.  If you need a
DLL you may take it from from the updated Gpg4win version.
For statically linked binaries remmeber to build them against GPGME
2.3.1.
If for some reasons GPGME can't be updated, you may patch your GPGME
version using the attached patch which likley applies cleanly even to
very old versions.
There is no need to udpate GPGMEPP or GPGMEQT.
Noteworthy changes in version 2.3.1 (2026-11-19)
================================================
         [compared to version 2.2.0]
 * New constants GPGME_PK_MLKEM, GPGME_PK_MLDSA, and GPGME_PK_DLTHM.
   Add mappings for algos used bei backends.   [rMa85fa49236]
 * Fix a double free in the status line read code.
   [T8557,CVE-2026-129975]
 * Interface changes relative to the 2.2.0 release:
 ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
 GPGME_PK_DLTHM                NEW.
 GPGME_PK_MLKEM                NEW.
 GPGME_PK_MLDSA                NEW.
 Release-info: https://dev.gnupg.org/T8427
Getting the Software
====================
Please follow the instructions found at <https://gnupg.org/download/> or
read on:
The GPGME source code compressed using BZIP2 and its OpenPGP signature
are available here:
 https://gnupg.org/ftp/gcrypt/gpgme/gpgme-2.3.1.tar.bz2 (1393k)
 https://gnupg.org/ftp/gcrypt/gpgme/gpgme-2.3.1.tar.bz2.sig
A sufficient patch to solve the security bug for older versions is
attached.   Note that version 2.3.0 was never publicly available.
Debian Packages
===============
We also provide Debian style packages for a couple of Debian variants.
See https://repos.gnupg.org/deb/gnupg/trixie/ or use the menu to switch
to other distros/releases.
Windows Installer
=================
A new version of Gpg4win (5.1.2), our full featured Windows installer,
including the fixed version of GPGME, the Kleopatra GUI, and a PDF
reader has also been released.  Head over to https://gpg4win.org/
Checking the Integrity
======================
See https://gnupg.org/download/integrity_check.html
The checksum for gpgme is:
1134a407b50204ee6fb9efaa6e1451c2460e543e  gpgme-2.3.1.tar.bz2
*Thank you all*
   Your GnuPG hackers
p.s.
This is an announcement only mailing list.  Please send replies only to
the gnupg-users at gnupg.org mailing list.

* Debian Package Signing Key:
  The new Debian style packages are signed using this key:
    ed25519 2025-07-08 [SC] [expires: 2035-07-14]
    3209 7B71 9B37 45D6 E61D DA1B 85C4 5AE3 E1A2 B355
    GnuPG.org Package Signing Key <package-maintainers at gnupg.org>
  See the package website (https://repos.gnupg.org/deb/gnupg) for a list
  of supported distributions and a download link for the key.

RCE in Gnupg
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
08:32:58 UTC
17:32:58 KST

[Announce] GnuPG Security Advisory for GPGME (T8557,CVE-2026-129975)
Werner Koch 
    wk at gnupg.org
       
Fri Nov 20 19:01:51 CET 2026
    
    
Hello!
We are pleased to announce the availability of a new GPGME release:
version 2.3.1.  This version fixes a *critical security bug* found in
all prior versions.  We also release a new Gpg4win versions and and
GnuPG Desktop and GnuPG VS-Desktop versions which all make use of GPGME.
Description
===========
By using an OpenPGP key containing a crafted very long user id it is
possible to trigger a double free.  This double free can be used
exfiltrate data from applications by sending for example signed data.
Exploiting this bug to mount an Remote Code Execution attack (RCE) can't
be ruled out.  Thus an immediate update is highly suggested.
Our internal ID is T8557 (https://static.dev.gnupg.org/T8557)
The CVE-id is CVE-2026-129975.
This vulnerability was discovered by Johan Carlsson and we were informed
by GitLab on 2026-11-12.  Initially we set 2026-11-25 for a coordinated
disclosure; due to the new Ruby2Shell bug GitLab had to update earlier
and thus we also disclosed the GPGME bug on 2026-11-20.
The root cause for this bug are two GPGME internal functions to read the
status lines and key listings from gpg.  Initially a buffer of 1024
bytes is allocated which is reallocated if more space is required.  When
reallocating the pointer to the new buffer is used by the function but
it is only assigned back to the context object at the end of the
function.  Thus if the function returns early due to an read error or
other events the context is not updated and keeps the stale pointer to
the freed memory.  Eventually the context is released and the memory
freed a second time.  Due to other allocations in between the common
double free checks of memory allocators may not be triggered.
Impact
======
All software using a GPGME version prior to 2.3.0 either as shared
libary, DLL, or statically linked is affected.  Examples of software
using GPGME are
 - Gpg4win
 - GnuPG Desktop
 - GnuPG VS-Desktop
 - Most mail clients
 - Many XMPP clients
 - Samba
 - Flatpack
Solution
========
Please update your GPGME shared library to version 2.3.1.  If you need a
DLL you may take it from from the updated Gpg4win version.
For statically linked binaries remmeber to build them against GPGME
2.3.1.
If for some reasons GPGME can't be updated, you may patch your GPGME
version using the attached patch which likley applies cleanly even to
very old versions.
There is no need to udpate GPGMEPP or GPGMEQT.
Noteworthy changes in version 2.3.1 (2026-11-19)
================================================
         [compared to version 2.2.0]
 * New constants GPGME_PK_MLKEM, GPGME_PK_MLDSA, and GPGME_PK_DLTHM.
   Add mappings for algos used bei backends.   [rMa85fa49236]
 * Fix a double free in the status line read code.
   [T8557,CVE-2026-129975]
 * Interface changes relative to the 2.2.0 release:
 ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
 GPGME_PK_DLTHM                NEW.
 GPGME_PK_MLKEM                NEW.
 GPGME_PK_MLDSA                NEW.
 Release-info: https://dev.gnupg.org/T8427
Getting the Software
====================
Please follow the instructions found at <https://gnupg.org/download/> or
read on:
The GPGME source code compressed using BZIP2 and its OpenPGP signature
are available here:
 https://gnupg.org/ftp/gcrypt/gpgme/gpgme-2.3.1.tar.bz2 (1393k)
 https://gnupg.org/ftp/gcrypt/gpgme/gpgme-2.3.1.tar.bz2.sig
A sufficient patch to solve the security bug for older versions is
attached.   Note that version 2.3.0 was never publicly available.
Debian Packages
===============
We also provide Debian style packages for a couple of Debian variants.
See https://repos.gnupg.org/deb/gnupg/trixie/ or use the menu to switch
to other distros/releases.
Windows Installer
=================
A new version of Gpg4win (5.1.2), our full featured Windows installer,
including the fixed version of GPGME, the Kleopatra GUI, and a PDF
reader has also been released.  Head over to https://gpg4win.org/
Checking the Integrity
======================
See https://gnupg.org/download/integrity_check.html
The checksum for gpgme is:
1134a407b50204ee6fb9efaa6e1451c2460e543e  gpgme-2.3.1.tar.bz2
*Thank you all*
   Your GnuPG hackers

Exascale fusion simulation team named finalist for 2026 Gordon Bell Prize
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
08:32:34 UTC
17:32:34 KST

A multi-institutional team leveraged ALCF's Aurora and OLCF's Frontier systems to perform exascale simulations of a liquid-metal fusion breeding blanket, demonstrating new modeling capabilities that could help advance the design of future fusion reactors.
Using two U.S. Department of Energy’s (DOE) exascale supercomputers devoted to open science, a multi-institutional team has carried out the largest and most detailed simulations to date of a critical component for fusion energy systems. The work has been named a finalist for the Association for Computing Machinery’s prestigious Gordon Bell Prize, which recognizes outstanding achievement in high-performance computing.
The simulations targeted a key part of many proposed fusion reactor designs: the breeding blanket. Located around the plasma in a fusion reactor, breeding blankets are designed to capture energy from fusion reactions, help shield reactor components from damage, and produce tritium, a scarce hydrogen isotope needed as fuel.
The team zeroed in on the water-cooled lead-lithium (WCLL) blanket design for ITER, the international fusion experiment under construction in France. Their simulations captured several processes interacting across the full blanket, including liquid-metal flow under strong magnetic fields, heat transfer, buoyancy, tritium transport and water cooling.
“We’re tackling a very challenging multiphysics, multiscale problem involving fluid systems with very different properties,” said project lead Elia Merzari, a professor of nuclear engineering and mechanical engineering at Pennsylvania State University with a joint appointment at DOE’s Argonne National Laboratory. “This is the first time a blanket has been modeled at this fidelity, including all the physics.”
Meeting that challenge required expertise in both fusion energy science and high-performance computing, along with access to two of the world’s most powerful supercomputers. The project brought together researchers from Argonne, Penn State, the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign and the UK Atomic Energy Authority.
To carry out the massive simulations, the team used Aurora at the Argonne Leadership Computing Facility (ALCF) and Frontier at the Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility (OLCF). Both are exascale supercomputers capable of performing quintillions of calculations per second. The ALCF and OLCF are DOE Office of Science user facilities supported by DOE’s Advanced Scientific Computing Research program.
Fusion blankets are highly complex systems. In a WCLL blanket, liquid lead-lithium flows through breeding zones, where lithium interacts with neutrons to produce tritium. Separate water circuits remove heat. Because the liquid metal is electrically conductive, its flow is strongly affected by the powerful magnetic fields used to confine plasma in a reactor.
Interactions between the liquid metal and the magnetic field can create extremely thin boundary layers — regions where the flow changes sharply over a very short distance. Those tiny features exist within a blanket that spans roughly a meter, creating an enormous difference between the largest and smallest scales in the flow. At the same time, water can move through the system hundreds of times faster than the liquid metal, so the simulation must also account for processes occurring at very different rates. Capturing this wide range of spatial and temporal scales required a high-resolution multiscale model and exascale computing power.
With access to Aurora and Frontier, the team’s calculations used more than 2 billion spectral elements and 2 trillion grid points, making them the largest liquid-metal magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) simulations to date. Spectral elements divide the blanket model into smaller regions that represent its geometry. Grid points are locations within those regions where the model calculates conditions such as velocity and temperature.
“Modeling two trillion grid points is something you can only do with exascale computing,” Merzari said. “The need for that resolution is really driven by the physics. You need very high resolution to capture the thin boundary layers in the flow created by MHD.”
The simulations allowed the researchers to examine how these physical effects influence blanket behavior. One key finding was that MHD suppresses turbulence and reorganize the liquid-metal flow, affecting pressure, temperature and heat transfer. The team also observed that temperature differences in the liquid can drive buoyancy, further altering the flow, especially near the wall facing the plasma. Understanding these complex interactions can help researchers improve fusion blanket designs.
Carrying out simulations at this scale required years of code development and validation. The team used NekRS, an open-source computational fluid dynamics code developed at Argonne, for the high-fidelity multiphysics simulations. NekRS builds on decades of advances in the Nek5000 code, which has supported past Gordon Bell Prize-winning research and demonstrated high performance and scalability for incompressible flow simulations on CPU-based supercomputers. NekRS extends that foundation by adapting and improving algorithms for GPUs. The team also used the open-source OpenMC code to calculate neutron transport, providing data on heating, tritium production and water activation as inputs to the NekRS simulations.
To prepare for the exascale runs, the researchers expanded NekRS to model MHD effects and optimized the code for simulations of this scale. This effort was led by Yu-Hsiang Lan, a doctoral student at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign. He worked closely with two mentors and longtime NekRS developers, Paul Fischer of University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign and Argonne, and Misun Min of Argonne.
“My role was to develop the solver that brings MHD into NekRS and validate it against similar problems in the literature to make sure everything was working correctly before we pushed it to larger scales,” Lan said.
Lan also implemented a capability known as global mesh refinement in NekRS. This allowed the team to start with a lower-resolution model and progressively add detail rather than building the largest model from the outset.
“In terms of workflow, this was a lifesaver,” Merzari said. “It really helped us get this work done in a fraction of the time.”
NekRS’s ability to run on different types of GPUs allowed the team to perform and scale the simulations on both Frontier and Aurora. Large-scale supercomputers are made up of thousands of interconnected computers, called nodes, that work together to perform complex calculations. The team ran simulations on more than 9,000 nodes on each system, using nearly the full capacity of both machines. They also demonstrated strong scaling on both systems, showing that increasing the number of nodes continued to reduce simulation time.
Frontier served as a primary platform for code development, testing and validation. The system also supported in-situ visualization using the open-source Ascent software. This capability allowed researchers to capture simulation data at high frequency as the calculations ran, enabling them to study transient flow behavior in greater detail and generate high-quality movies of the evolving flow.
The researchers turned to Aurora for their largest simulation, leveraging its substantial memory capacity to run the complete multiphysics model, including MHD, at a resolution of 2 trillion grid points.
High-fidelity simulations provide a valuable complement to fusion experiments, allowing researchers to study and predict how fusion systems behave under extreme conditions.
“These types of experiments are very hard and also very expensive,” Lan said. “Simulation is an important tool for design and can help reduce the cost of actual experiments.”
The detailed data from these simulations can also help researchers develop and test new approaches to fusion engineering. One longer-term goal is to use simulations of different designs and operating conditions to develop faster models that require less computing power.
“Now that we’ve shown this is doable, people can use the results or the code as a tool to validate a low-fidelity model,” Lan said. “Then the low-fidelity model eventually can be used for things like AI design optimization or digital twins.”
Lan is already contributing to a separate Argonne-led project that is building on this work, using data from the blanket simulations to develop simplified system-analysis tools for fusion applications.
More broadly, the team’s exascale simulations mark another step forward for fusion modeling. Their scale and level of detail provide new insights that can help accelerate the path to practical fusion energy.
“There is such interesting physics in simulations at this scale and so much we can learn as we continue working to develop better fusion energy technologies,” Merzari said.
This research is detailed in the paper “Exascale Multiphysics Simulations for Liquid Metal Fusion Blankets,” which will be presented Nov. 17 as part of the ACM Gordon Bell Finalists Presentations session at SC26. In addition to Elia Merzari and Yu-Hsiang Lan, the paper's authors are Misun Min, Haomin Yuan, Patrick Shriwise, Stefan Kerkemeier, Kristopher Rowe and Ramesh Balakrishnan of Argonne National Laboratory; Paul Fischer of Argonne and the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign; Tri Nguyen, Maximiliano Dalinger and Luiz Aldeia Machado of Penn State University; Ananias Tomboulides of Aristotle University; and Andrew Davis, Aleksander Dubas and Rupert Eardley-Brunt of the UK Atomic Energy Authority.
The team received computing time on Aurora and Frontier through DOE’s Innovative and Novel Computational Impact on Theory and Experiment (INCITE) program, which is supported by the Office of Advanced Scientific Computing Research. Development of Nek5000 and NekRS was supported by ASCR’s Applied Mathematics program and DOE’s Nuclear Energy Advanced Modeling and Simulation program.
Argonne National Laboratory seeks solutions to pressing national problems in science and technology by conducting leading-edge basic and applied research in virtually every scientific discipline. Argonne is managed by UChicago Argonne, LLC for the U.S. Department of Energy’s Office of Science.
UT-Battelle LLC manages ORNL for DOE’s Office of Science, the single largest supporter of basic research in the physical sciences in the United States. The Office of Science is working to address some of the most pressing challenges of our time. For more information, please visit https://energy.gov/science.

Anthropic Mathematician Claims to Prove Riemann Hypothesis
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

Anthropic Mathematician Claims to Prove Riemann Hypothesis (scientificamerican.com) 82
			
		 	
				Slashdot has been covering the Riemann Hypothesis for years.
2000: Prove The Riemann Hypothesis And Make.Money.Fast 
2002: More on Riemann Hypothesis 
2008: Claimed Proof of Riemann Hypothesis 
2008: Prominent Mathematicians Rebuke Recent Riemann Hypothesis Proof 
Add a new headline to the list...
2026:  Using AI, mathematician claims to have solved one of math's great mysteries 
joshuark shares a report from Scientific American: Levent Alpoge, a mathematician at Anthropic, has claimed to have solved the Riemann Hypothesis with the help of Anthropic's AI model Claude, but the proof is not yet independently verified and remains under scrutiny. [An AI-generated solution of the 167-year-old problem, long considered far out of reach to human mathematicians, would cap off the technology's year-long conquest of math in spectacular fashion.] The Riemann hypothesis was first proposed in 1859 by the mathematician Bernhard Riemann. It claims that the locations of the prime numbers along the number line, which appear random at first blush, are actually determined by an intricate, meaningful order.
[...] Alpoge posted his Claude proof coded in the programming language Lean, which verifies the mathematical correctness of each argument. Issues can arise in Lean, but it shouldn't take long for experts to verify whether the hypothesis is properly coded, and if the proof is correct. If that happens, it will cap off a year in which AI's mathematical capabilities ascended from unreliable college student to a superhuman force, leaving no part of the discipline undisrupted. [...]  Alpoge's statement is elegiac -- recognizing the sense of grief many mathematicians are feeling -- but also optimistic. "It is natural to feel that this is an end, in my case a loss of a principal guiding light," he wrote, "but what I saw during the course of progress so amazed me that I believe it is one dawn across science of something far greater." [...]
The Riemann result, if correct, will echo far beyond mathematics. It doesn't have any immediate real-world applications -- it won't upend global banking or make the internet run faster. But after a year of watching LLMs surpass one math benchmark after another, there is no more staggering achievement it could possibly reach. A machine may have just solved a problem that the smartest people on the planet all agreed they were nowhere close to figuring out. As MIT mathematician Andrew Sutherland once put it to me: "An AI that can prove the Riemann hypothesis is not one I'd want to meet. If that happens, mathematicians having jobs will be the least of our problems."
"If the proof is correct and understood, this completes a dictionary between different branches of math where the connections were only partial," says Hector Pasten, a mathematician at the Pontifical Catholic University of Chile. "Something like a Rosetta Stone."
"If true, even from a first high level scan of the summary of the proposed argument, it is very plausible to me that the new ideas in the proof will open up brand new avenues of thinking in number theory and have an immense impact beside the obviously extremely influential result in itself," added mathematician Carlo Pagano, a professor at Concordia University and a visiting researcher at Google DeepMind.
In a separate article from Scientific American, independent mathematicians say the AI-assisted proof is almost certainly valid. MIT mathematician Andrew Sutherland said the chance of anything being wrong was "approximately zero" after independently compiling and checking the code. However, researchers are still conducting final checks to ensure the proof accurately represents the original hypothesis and does not exploit any loopholes in Lean's verification system.
But did a human beat the AI?  The Washington Post reports:
Just hours after Alpoge's claim circulated on social media, Ravi Vakil, president of the American Mathematical Society and a professor at Stanford University, said in a hurried statement that mathematician Alessandro Masullo had a proposed proof of the Riemann hypothesis without AI several months earlier. The draft, which runs some 940 pages, is in need of significant revisions, Masullo said in a statement, and was not ready for publication. Online observers quickly noted that it contained omissions and apparent errors.
But in the introduction to the draft, he published a striking flag.
"If any message at all this work is meant to send to the mathematician who feels discouraged by the pace and tone of the current climate, it is this," he wrote. "We are still here. We are very much alive. We are not going anywhere."
