ClawDOS – An AI coding agent for DOS
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
05:38:01 UTC
14:38:01 KST

An AI coding agent for DOS.
It runs on an IBM PC XT: an 8088 at 4.77 MHz, 640K of RAM, a CGA card.
    A (real) human’s note:
Backstory: The IBM PC was my first computer. Rather, it was my parents’ first personal computer that I got to use at a very early age.
It was already obsolete by the time I was old enough to use it, but at 5 years old it was a magical device that I could make do different things depending on what I typed.
I learned how to type and use DOS so that I could launch some early “Learn the Alphabet” games that my mom had bought me. I learned some elementary-level use of BASIC, but at the time that didn’t progress much further than making some number guessing games and other small projects.
From there, many years passed but I still have some of the original parts of that PC. I have a collection of vintage machines that mean something to me, with my fully maxed-out IBM PC 5160 being the centerpiece of my collection. It still has a working 20 MB hard drive, but that’s disconnected in favor of a PicoMEM that lets me emulate a hard drive off of an SD card, making experimentation and play much easier.
Old, meet new. New, meet old: I love intersections of old and new technology. What better way to cross the two than building an AI coding agent to run on DOS on original hardware?
And of course, what better to build it than an AI coding agent?
I (read: Claude) built this for my own entertainment. I wanted to see how far I could push an AI coding agent to build something given out of the (modern) norm of constraints. Also, can that something it built then build other somethings? 100% of this repo is written by Claude, with the exception of the text within this box.
As explained in more detail below, the harness runs on the native OS and hardware and relies on a “bridge” PC in order to handle TLS, which would’ve been more than what an 8088 could handle.
Type CLAWDOS at the C:\> prompt and talk to Claude. It reads and edits the
files on your disk, writes programs, compiles them with Turbo C, runs them and
looks at what they draw on the screen. It keeps notes between conversations and
picks up where it left off.
The whole harness runs on the PC. HARNESS.EXE is a 176 KB DOS program that
speaks the Claude Messages API itself: it writes the requests, parses the
streamed reply, runs the tools and keeps every conversation on its own disk.
The 8088 does everything but TLS.
Every screen below comes from a 5160 at 4.77 MHz, emulated in 86Box. The conversations are with the live API.
One message: “Write PRIMES.C: print the primes below 100. Compile it with Turbo C and run it.” ClawDOS reads its guide to the compilers on this machine, writes the source, then compiles, links and runs it in a single tool call. The answer is on the screen 64 seconds after Enter, 26 of them spent compiling, linking and running.
The compiler gets the machine to itself. For a build, ClawDOS saves its state and leaves memory, Turbo C runs with about 450K free, and the harness comes back on the same conversation with the program’s output in hand.
ClawDOS wrote this tribute to the 1983 film on the PC itself: a vector world
map, missiles arcing over the pole, a tic-tac-toe endgame, and a score for the
PC speaker or an AdLib card. Five files of Turbo C, 15 KB of source, a
WG.EXE under 10 KB.
Here it is running on the real 5160, filmed off its IBM monitor:
  
▶ Watch the WarGames demo on YouTube
Start a new conversation and “your WarGames demo” is all it needs. ClawDOS
opens the note it saved in C:\MEMORY, finds the latest build, and recalls
the palette trick the war scene plays on the CGA.
That Run call asked for a screenshot 25 seconds in. A timer-interrupt hook
copies the CGA’s video memory at that moment, the 8088 encodes it as a PNG, and
the picture goes to Claude with the tool result. This is the file the PC made,
320x200 in four colours, 16 KB:
And this is what Claude made of it:
Asked to make the demo exit on its own, ClawDOS goes looking. It searches the sources for the keyboard wait, reads the file, says what it found and edits one line. Each tool call shows in the transcript with its result, and between them Claude says what it is about to do.
Text art in sixteen colours, straight into the transcript, and music on the PC
speaker in GW-BASIC’s PLAY language.
A chat screen built for the CGA. Replies stream onto the screen as they arrive, word-wrapped at 80 columns in code page 437. Writes are timed to the retrace, so there is no snow. The transcript scrolls in hardware through the CRTC start address, with PgUp and PgDn to go back. The spinner and its seconds run from the timer interrupt and keep moving while the PC is busy.
Eighteen tools. Claude works on the machine through these:
A development kit on the disk. Two assemblers (TINYASM and WASM),
Bruce’s C, GW-BASIC and the lDebug debugger, plus Turbo C 2.0, installed from
your own floppy images in external/. All of them are proven on the 8088, and
ClawDOS knows how to drive each one.
Memories and skills. Memories are what ClawDOS learns on your PC: what you built together, what worked, what you like. Skills are guides that ship with it, such as the dev tools and CGA demo effects. Both are plain text files on the disk, listed in every conversation’s system prompt and read when they fit the task. F4 browses the memories:
Safe mode. On by default. Anything that changes a file, memory or a port
asks Allow ...? (y/n) first. F3 turns it off.
Conversations that last. Every chat is a file on the disk in the API’s own message format. F6 lists them, Enter reopens one, and your next message carries it on. A chat cut off by the power switch reopens too.
Any model, mid-conversation. /model lists the providers in
CLAWDOS.CFG and switches between them and their models without losing the
conversation. F5 sets how hard Claude thinks, from low to max. Any host that
speaks the Messages API works.
Easy on a slow link. The system prompt and tools are cached by the API. After a conversation’s first request the PC uploads only what is new, so a tool result goes up in about 500 bytes. A dropped connection or an overloaded API is retried, and the turn carries on.
A flight recorder. CLAWDOS.LOG has a line for every round: time to first
token, upload size and time, tokens in and out, cache reads.
api anthropic claude-opus-5-5 round=1 ttft=2750 total=4015 up=165 len=1784 stop=tool_use in=2 out=221 cr=5543 cw=739 th=cont
relaunch: program 61.5 s, CLAWDOS away 82.3 s
flowchart LR
    subgraph DOS["IBM PC XT (8088, 640K, CGA)"]
        direction TB
        subgraph APP["HARNESS.EXE"]
            direction TB
            UI["Chat screen"]
            LOOP["Agent loop<br/>Messages API requests, SSE and JSON,<br/>history on disk"]
            TOOLS["Tools<br/>files, search, DOS programs, screenshots,<br/>memory and ports, speaker"]
        end
        NET["mTCP + packet driver"]
        APP <--> NET
    end
    TUNNEL["TLS tunnel<br/>copies bytes"]
    API["Claude Messages API"]
    NET <-->|"plain HTTP"| TUNNEL
    TUNNEL <-->|"HTTPS"| API
docs/API.md has exactly what ClawDOS sends and what it expects back.
You need a Mac or a Windows PC with Python 3, and an Anthropic API key. On
Windows the command is python.
python3 scripts/setup-tools.py          # fetch the compilers, FreeDOS, mTCP and the emulators
echo ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=sk-ant-... > .env
python3 scripts/build.py                # build/CLAWDOS.IMG, a bootable disk
python3 tunnel/tunnel.py                # the TLS tunnel, on port 8443
python3 scripts/run86box.py             # boot an IBM PC XT
At the C:\> prompt, type CLAWDOS.
No key? python3 tunnel/mockapi.py plays the API offline: type
CLAWDOS 10.0.2.2:18023 and send !help.
On a real PC: build with the address of the machine running the tunnel
(python3 scripts/build.py --tunnel 192.168.1.10:8443), copy CLAWDOS.IMG to
the PicoMEM’s microSD card, pick it as the hard disk in the PicoMEM’s menu,
boot, and type CLAWDOS. Later builds update the card’s image in place and
keep its memories and chats
(python3 scripts/build.py --update /path/to/CLAWDOS.IMG).
/model, /chats, /memories, /new and /quit work from the input box.
CLAWDOS -m "..." sends a message from the command line and -c NAME reopens
a chat.
ClawDOS is free software under the GNU General Public License, version 3 or (at your option)
any later version: see LICENSE. HARNESS.EXE links mTCP, which is GPLv3. The FreeDOS
boot sector in src/boot/freedos is vendored unmodified under its own GPLv2-or-later license
(its COPYING). Disk images you build hold other people’s software (FreeDOS, mTCP, and Turbo C
from your own floppies): don’t redistribute an image with Turbo C on it.
ClawDOS stands on mTCP, FreeDOS, Open Watcom, the PicoMEM, 86Box and DOSBox-X.

ClawDOS – An AI coding agent for DOS
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
05:38:01 UTC
14:38:01 KST

An AI coding agent for DOS.
It runs on an IBM PC XT: an 8088 at 4.77 MHz, 640K of RAM, a CGA card.
    A (real) human’s note:
Backstory: The IBM PC was my first computer. Rather, it was my parents’ first personal computer that I got to use at a very early age.
It was already obsolete by the time I was old enough to use it, but at 5 years old it was a magical device that I could make do different things depending on what I typed.
I learned how to type and use DOS so that I could launch some early “Learn the Alphabet” games that my mom had bought me. I learned some elementary-level use of BASIC, but at the time that didn’t progress much further than making some number guessing games and other small projects.
From there, many years passed but I still have some of the original parts of that PC. I have a collection of vintage machines that mean something to me, with my fully maxed-out IBM PC 5160 being the centerpiece of my collection. It still has a working 20 MB hard drive, but that’s disconnected in favor of a PicoMEM that lets me emulate a hard drive off of an SD card, making experimentation and play much easier.
Old, meet new. New, meet old: I love intersections of old and new technology. What better way to cross the two than building an AI coding agent to run on DOS on original hardware?
And of course, what better to build it than an AI coding agent?
I (read: Claude) built this for my own entertainment. I wanted to see how far I could push an AI coding agent to build something given out of the (modern) norm of constraints. Also, can that something it built then build other somethings? 100% of this repo is written by Claude, with the exception of the text within this box.
As explained in more detail below, the harness runs on the native OS and hardware and relies on a “bridge” PC in order to handle TLS, which would’ve been more than what an 8088 could handle.
Type CLAWDOS at the C:\> prompt and talk to Claude. It reads and edits the
files on your disk, writes programs, compiles them with Turbo C, runs them and
looks at what they draw on the screen. It keeps notes between conversations and
picks up where it left off.
The whole harness runs on the PC. HARNESS.EXE is a 176 KB DOS program that
speaks the Claude Messages API itself: it writes the requests, parses the
streamed reply, runs the tools and keeps every conversation on its own disk.
The 8088 does everything but TLS.
Every screen below comes from a 5160 at 4.77 MHz, emulated in 86Box. The conversations are with the live API.
One message: “Write PRIMES.C: print the primes below 100. Compile it with Turbo C and run it.” ClawDOS reads its guide to the compilers on this machine, writes the source, then compiles, links and runs it in a single tool call. The answer is on the screen 64 seconds after Enter, 26 of them spent compiling, linking and running.
The compiler gets the machine to itself. For a build, ClawDOS saves its state and leaves memory, Turbo C runs with about 450K free, and the harness comes back on the same conversation with the program’s output in hand.
ClawDOS wrote this tribute to the 1983 film on the PC itself: a vector world
map, missiles arcing over the pole, a tic-tac-toe endgame, and a score for the
PC speaker or an AdLib card. Five files of Turbo C, 15 KB of source, a
WG.EXE under 10 KB.
Here it is running on the real 5160, filmed off its IBM monitor:
  
▶ Watch the WarGames demo on YouTube
Start a new conversation and “your WarGames demo” is all it needs. ClawDOS
opens the note it saved in C:\MEMORY, finds the latest build, and recalls
the palette trick the war scene plays on the CGA.
That Run call asked for a screenshot 25 seconds in. A timer-interrupt hook
copies the CGA’s video memory at that moment, the 8088 encodes it as a PNG, and
the picture goes to Claude with the tool result. This is the file the PC made,
320x200 in four colours, 16 KB:
And this is what Claude made of it:
Asked to make the demo exit on its own, ClawDOS goes looking. It searches the sources for the keyboard wait, reads the file, says what it found and edits one line. Each tool call shows in the transcript with its result, and between them Claude says what it is about to do.
Text art in sixteen colours, straight into the transcript, and music on the PC
speaker in GW-BASIC’s PLAY language.
A chat screen built for the CGA. Replies stream onto the screen as they arrive, word-wrapped at 80 columns in code page 437. Writes are timed to the retrace, so there is no snow. The transcript scrolls in hardware through the CRTC start address, with PgUp and PgDn to go back. The spinner and its seconds run from the timer interrupt and keep moving while the PC is busy.
Eighteen tools. Claude works on the machine through these:
A development kit on the disk. Two assemblers (TINYASM and WASM),
Bruce’s C, GW-BASIC and the lDebug debugger, plus Turbo C 2.0, installed from
your own floppy images in external/. All of them are proven on the 8088, and
ClawDOS knows how to drive each one.
Memories and skills. Memories are what ClawDOS learns on your PC: what you built together, what worked, what you like. Skills are guides that ship with it, such as the dev tools and CGA demo effects. Both are plain text files on the disk, listed in every conversation’s system prompt and read when they fit the task. F4 browses the memories:
Safe mode. On by default. Anything that changes a file, memory or a port
asks Allow ...? (y/n) first. F3 turns it off.
Conversations that last. Every chat is a file on the disk in the API’s own message format. F6 lists them, Enter reopens one, and your next message carries it on. A chat cut off by the power switch reopens too.
Any model, mid-conversation. /model lists the providers in
CLAWDOS.CFG and switches between them and their models without losing the
conversation. F5 sets how hard Claude thinks, from low to max. Any host that
speaks the Messages API works.
Easy on a slow link. The system prompt and tools are cached by the API. After a conversation’s first request the PC uploads only what is new, so a tool result goes up in about 500 bytes. A dropped connection or an overloaded API is retried, and the turn carries on.
A flight recorder. CLAWDOS.LOG has a line for every round: time to first
token, upload size and time, tokens in and out, cache reads.
api anthropic claude-opus-5-5 round=1 ttft=2750 total=4015 up=165 len=1784 stop=tool_use in=2 out=221 cr=5543 cw=739 th=cont
relaunch: program 61.5 s, CLAWDOS away 82.3 s
flowchart LR
    subgraph DOS["IBM PC XT (8088, 640K, CGA)"]
        direction TB
        subgraph APP["HARNESS.EXE"]
            direction TB
            UI["Chat screen"]
            LOOP["Agent loop<br/>Messages API requests, SSE and JSON,<br/>history on disk"]
            TOOLS["Tools<br/>files, search, DOS programs, screenshots,<br/>memory and ports, speaker"]
        end
        NET["mTCP + packet driver"]
        APP <--> NET
    end
    TUNNEL["TLS tunnel<br/>copies bytes"]
    API["Claude Messages API"]
    NET <-->|"plain HTTP"| TUNNEL
    TUNNEL <-->|"HTTPS"| API

Startup sues Apple in India over tap-to-share contacts feature
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
04:00:00 UTC
13:00:00 KST

Apple is being sued in India by a U.S.-based startup that alleges the iPhone maker’s NameDrop feature, which lets users share contact information by bringing devices close together, infringes its patented technology.
GG Technologies, the startup behind StayTouch, an app that lets users exchange contact details by bringing their phones close together, is seeking ₹15 billion (about $161.86 million) in damages and an injunction against Apple, per court documents reviewed by TechCrunch.
Santa Monica-headquartered GG Technologies filed the lawsuit in India in October after mediation proceedings initiated in April failed in September, the startup told TechCrunch. Pre-institution mediation is generally required before filing commercial lawsuits in India. The company also sued Apple in the U.S. in April over related patent infringement claims.
In its 781-page court filing in the Bombay High Court, GG Technologies alleged that Apple’s NameDrop infringes its Indian patent on technology that helps phones exchange contact details with nearby devices without requiring manual pairing. The company launched StayTouch in 2019, the same year it first sought patent protection for the technology in the U.S. It filed its Indian patent application in 2021 and received the patent in July 2024.
Apple introduced NameDrop as part of iOS 17 in June 2023. The feature debuted on iPhones later that year, with support for compatible Apple Watches arriving in October through watchOS 10.1.
“We are seeking an injunction to restrict the usage of the NameDrop feature which is native to iOS,” GG Technologies told TechCrunch. GG Technologies urged the court in its filing to restrict Apple from selling or importing iPhones and Apple Watches preloaded with NameDrop in India. The case has yet to be listed for a hearing.
In the U.S., the International Trade Commission opened an investigation into Apple’s alleged patent infringement in August, following a complaint from GG Technologies. The startup is seeking an order that could block imports of infringing Apple devices into the country, along with restrictions on their sale.
Founded in 2018 by Gaurav Goel, a graduate of the Indian Institute of Technology (IIT) Delhi and a former Goldman Sachs investment banker, GG Technologies developed StayTouch as an alternative to exchanging business cards. The app is available on iOS and Android.
Apple did not respond to a request for comment.
India has become increasingly important to Apple, both as a fast-growing consumer market and a major manufacturing hub outside China. The Cupertino company has expanded its retail presence in the South Asian nation, where iPhones still account for less than 10% of smartphone shipments, giving considerable room for growth.

FCC Backs AT&amp;T's Plan To Remove Copper Landlines In California
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

FCC Backs AT&T's Plan To Remove Copper Landlines In California (pcmag.com) 77
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from PCMag: On Friday, the FCC announced that it was preempting "specific requirements in California law that prevent consumers from transitioning off of unreliable, slow copper lines," even as some residents fear they'll be saddled with spotty cell service as an alternative. AT&T argues that California's "Carrier of the Last Resort" (COLR) rules prevent it from removing the old-school copper network that currently serves only 3% of California households, a number that continues to shrink. In May, the company urged the commission to declare that federal laws preempted California's regulations, thus clearing the way for AT&T to discontinue the service as it prioritizes high-speed fiber and expanded 5G cellular coverage.
Friday's FCC order (PDF) partially grants AT&T's petition, agreeing that under federal law it has "explicit jurisdiction over the discontinuance of interstate telecommunications services." Still, the order refrains from "wholly" preempting California's Carrier of the Last Resort rules, and seems to instead focus on the AT&T situation. "While we preempt those requirements that necessitate that AT&T obtain a variety of CPUC [California Public Utilities Commission] approvals before it can discontinue POTS [plain old telephone service] in accordance with federal authorization, we do not preempt the COLR and other obligations more generally," the 74-page document says.
[...] Despite the FCC's order, it's not entirely clear if AT&T will pursue the copper landline phase-out in California. In May, the carrier sued to overturn the local regulations and block the CPUC from enforcing them against AT&T, which noted it spends $1 billion each year to maintain the copper network. That court case remains ongoing. [...] According to the regulator, AT&T has proposed discontinuing the legacy telephone service in the state on or after June 1, 2027.

Under the General Data Protection Regulation (“GDPR”) and/or the United Kingdom General Data Protection Regulation (“UK GDPR”), we are required to have a valid legal basis for processing your personal information, and we must inform you of that legal basis. Our legal basis for collecting and using the personal information described above will depend on the specific personal information concerned and the context in which it is collected. Generally, we will collect personal information from you only when you have consented to such collection, when we need the personal information to perform our obligations under a contract with you (for example, when you create an account or use our services), or where the processing is in our legitimate business interests (for example, to detect and prevent fraud and to analyze usage for analytics, modeling, and business intelligence). In some cases, we may also have a legal obligation to collect personal information from you (for example, to verify your identity when accessing your account or services).
Where we rely on legitimate interests, we balance our interests against your interests, rights, and freedoms and apply appropriate safeguards to protect your personal data.
If we ask you to provide personal information to comply with a legal requirement or to enter into a contract with us, we will make this clear at the time we request that personal information. We will also tell you whether providing that personal information is mandatory or not (as well as the possible consequences if you do not provide your personal information). Similarly, if we collect and use your personal information, we will provide you with our legitimate business interests for such collection and use.
If you have questions about or need further information concerning the legal basis for which we collect and use your personal information, please contact us as provided in the Contact for More Information section below.
We use industry-standard methods of securing electronic databases of personal information. However, you should know that no company can fully eliminate security risks associated with personal information. To help protect yourself, please use a strong password, do not use the same password to access your Backblaze account that you use with other accounts or services, and protect your usernames and passwords to help prevent others from accessing your accounts and services.
Your personal information may be transferred to and processed in the United States and other countries where Backblaze and its service providers operate, which may have data protection laws that differ from those in your jurisdiction.
Where required by law, we implement appropriate safeguards for such transfers, including contractual protections and other legally recognized mechanisms. While your information is outside of your country of residence, it is subject to the laws of the jurisdiction in which it is held and may be subject to disclosure to governments, courts, law enforcement, or regulatory agencies pursuant to applicable law.
If you reside within the EEA or in the U.K., you have the following rights under applicable law, which you can exercise at any time:
Please see the Exercising Your Privacy Rights section below to exercise your privacy rights.
We will respond to your request within 30 days of receipt. Where a request is complex, or we have received a large number of requests, we may extend this period by a further two months, as permitted under applicable law. If we extend the response period, we will inform you within 30 days of receiving your request and explain the reason for the delay. We will not charge a fee for responding to your request unless the request is manifestly unfounded or excessive, in which case we may charge a reasonable fee or decline to respond.
If you have an account, you may log in to access, update, or delete your personal information and associated data. If you are unable to access your account, you may contact us using the information provided in the Contact for More Information section below.
In cases where we rely on your consent to process personal information, you can revoke your consent by contacting us. Revoking your consent does not affect the lawfulness of any processing conducted prior to your withdrawal.
You may manage your cookie and tracking preferences through the cookie and tracking preference center, which may be accessed by clicking the Cookie Settings link in the footer of our website or through any other cookie settings mechanism made available on the Platform. Please note that cookie and tracking preferences are generally browser- and device-specific, and data collected before we can identify or authenticate you may not be able to be associated with your account.
Backblaze does not make decisions about you that produce legal effects or similarly significant effects based solely on automated processing, including profiling. In particular, we do not use automated decision-making to make decisions about your eligibility for services, pricing, or access to functionality. This does not prevent us from using personal information for ordinary business purposes, such as analytics, lead management, marketing segmentation, customer relationship management, fraud prevention, security, or service improvement, where such processing does not produce legal or similarly significant effects concerning you.

Please read our Cookie Notice to learn about our practices regarding cookies and similar technologies.
You may access or modify your cookie and tracking preferences at any time by clicking the Cookie Settings link in the footer of our website. The exact choices available to you may depend on your location and the laws that apply to you.
Cookies: Cookies are small data files stored on your device when you visit. Cookies allow us to recognize your device during subsequent visits and personalize your experience based on your preferences. They may also provide information to our service providers to help us improve website functionality and usability.
The Platform may use first-party and third-party cookies, as well as other tracking technologies, for advertising and analytics purposes. First-party cookies are set directly by Backblaze and are used to make the Platform function properly, remember your preferences, and support our internal analytics. Third-party cookies are set by our partners and service providers, such as advertising networks and analytics providers, and may be used to track your activity across different websites to deliver targeted advertising and measure performance.
If you consent to the use of cookies by clicking “Accept All Cookies” when you first visit the website, we will collect certain information from you, including Internet Protocol (IP) addresses, browser types, operating systems, page requests, and average time spent on our website. This data helps us understand website activity, monitor performance, and improve the user experience.
You can set your browser to notify you when cookies are received or to block certain types of cookies. However, disabling cookies may limit your ability to access some features of the website. For additional details on how to opt out of the use of cookies, please review our Cookie Notice.
Analytics: We utilize tools like Google Analytics to collect data about website usage, including pages visited, time spent, search terms, and engagement metrics. This information helps us evaluate and enhance the user experience. For example, to opt out of Google Analytics, you can use the Google Analytics Opt-Out Browser Add-On. For more information about how Google uses your data, please visit “How Google uses data when you use our partners' sites or apps.” Please review our Cookie Notice for information on analytics tools used on the website.
Interest-Based Advertising: We work with third parties, such as advertisers, advertising networks, and data exchanges, to deliver tailored content and advertising based on your online activity. These third parties may use their own tracking technologies, including cookies and pixel tags, to collect information about your interactions with our website and other websites. This data helps deliver personalized advertisements for products or services that may interest you.
To opt out of interest-based advertising through cookies or similar technologies on our website, click the Cookie Settings link in the footer of our website and update your preferences in our cookie and tracking preference center. You may also use industry opt-out tools, such as Your Online Choices, where available. Please note that opting out does not mean you will no longer receive ads; instead, advertisements may no longer be tailored to your preferences based on online behavior. Cookie-based opt-outs are generally browser- and device-specific, so you may need to repeat the opt-out process if you use a different browser or device or clear your cookies.
Backblaze complies with the EU-U.S. Data Privacy Framework (EU-U.S. DPF) and the UK Extension to the EU-U.S. DPF, and the Swiss-U.S. Data Privacy Framework (Swiss-U.S. DPF) as set forth by the U.S. Department of Commerce. Backblaze has certified to the U.S. Department of Commerce that it adheres to the EU-U.S. Data Privacy Framework Principles (EU-U.S. DPF Principles) with regard to the processing of personal data received from the European Union and the United Kingdom in reliance on the EU-U.S. DPF and the UK Extension to the EU-U.S. DPF. Backblaze has certified to the U.S. Department of Commerce that it adheres to the Swiss-U.S. Data Privacy Framework Principles (Swiss-U.S. DPF Principles) with regard to the processing of personal data received from Switzerland in reliance on the Swiss-U.S. DPF. If there is any conflict between the terms in this privacy policy and the EU-U.S. DPF Principles and/or the Swiss-U.S. DPF Principles, the Principles shall govern. To learn more about the Data Privacy Framework (DPF) program and to view our certification, please visit https://www.dataprivacyframework.gov/.
In compliance with the EU-U.S. DPF and the UK Extension to the EU-U.S. DPF and the Swiss-U.S. DPF, Backblaze commits to refer unresolved complaints concerning our handling of personal data received in reliance on the EU-U.S. DPF and the UK Extension to the EU-U.S. DPF and the Swiss-U.S. DPF to the International Division of the American Arbitration Association (ICDR-AAA), an alternative dispute resolution provider based in the United States.
If you do not receive timely acknowledgment of your DPF Principles-related complaint from us, or if we have not addressed your DPF Principles-related complaint to your satisfaction, please visit https://feature.adr.org/dpf_irm for more information or to file a complaint. The services of ICDR-AAA are provided at no cost to you.

This Notice at Collection and Privacy Policy (“Privacy Notice”) describes how Backblaze, Inc. (“Backblaze,” the “Company,” “we,” “our,” or “us”) collects, uses, discloses, processes, and retains personal information (“Information Practices”). This Privacy Notice explains the privacy rights available to you under applicable privacy laws and how you can exercise those rights. We respect your privacy and are committed to providing a transparent notice of our privacy practices.
Backblaze is headquartered in the United States and is a data storage company that provides two primary services. The first, Computer Backup, offers unlimited cloud backup solutions for individuals and organizations using Mac or PC devices, including laptops and desktops. The second, B2 Cloud Storage, provides scalable cloud storage solutions designed to meet the needs of individuals and organizations.
This Privacy Notice explains how Backblaze collects, uses, and shares your personal information when Backblaze acts as a data controller, meaning Backblaze determines the purposes and methods of processing your personal information unless otherwise specified in a separate agreement or required by applicable law. Click here to learn more about the controller-processor distinction.
This Privacy Notice applies when you visit our website (www.Backblaze.com), use the Backblaze App, and all features, applications, social media channels, browser plugins, content, downloads, and other online services that we make available or that link to this Privacy Notice (together, the “Platform”). It also applies to personal information we collect directly from you when you interact with us, including when you communicate with us through emails, phone calls, texts, or other channels; use our products, services, and applications; engage in commercial transactions with us; or register for and participate in webinars, events, programs, marketing, and promotional activities. In addition, this Privacy Notice covers information we collect offline and information collected indirectly through third parties, including our partners, in the course of our business.
The terms used in the Privacy Notice have the same or similar meaning as those terms under applicable privacy laws, or the closest approximation of such terms.
We recognize that privacy and data protection requirements vary across jurisdictions, and we are committed to meeting our obligations under each applicable legal framework. Accordingly, this Privacy Notice sets forth our privacy practices on an organization-wide basis, and jurisdiction-specific components, obligations, and rights that may apply to individuals in particular states, regions or countries are described in specific sections below. In the event of any conflict between the general provisions of this Privacy Notice and a jurisdiction-specific section, the jurisdiction-specific section shall control to the extent of such conflict.
We encourage you to read this Privacy Notice in its entirety, including the jurisdiction-specific sections that may be relevant to you based on your location or residency, so that you understand the full scope of our data processing activities and the rights available to you under applicable law.
The following notice identifies the categories of personal information to be collected from you and the purposes for which the personal information will be used, including whether that information is sold or shared for the purposes of cross-context behavioral advertising (e.g., targeted advertising).
Most U.S. state comprehensive privacy laws apply only to personal information processed about individuals acting in an individual or household context and exclude personal information processed about individuals acting in a commercial or employment context. Where applicable state law excludes such information from the scope of consumer rights, those rights are not available through this Privacy Notice for that information. The California Consumer Privacy Act (“CCPA”) differs in that it extends certain rights to California employees and job applicants. If you are a Backblaze employee or job applicant, please refer to our separate Employee Privacy Notice here for information about how we collect, use, and protect your personal information in that context.
Note: We do not collect Biometric Information, Inferences and Profile Data, Professional or Employment-Related Information, or Education Information in the consumer context.
    
Note: We do not collect other sensitive personal information under applicable state laws.
The period of time for which we retain personal information depends on the purposes for which we collect and use it. We will delete or de-identify personal information when it is no longer needed to fulfill the purposes listed for each category, unless a longer retention period is required or permitted to: (a) comply with applicable laws, including tax, accounting, and recordkeeping laws; (b) establish, exercise, or defend legal claims; (c) prevent fraud and ensure the security of our services; or (d) fulfill another legitimate business purpose permitted by applicable state law. Where technical or operational limitations prevent complete deletion or de-identification, we will take reasonable measures to prevent further processing.
Our use of tracking technologies may be considered a “sale” or “sharing” of personal information with third parties for cross-context behavioral advertising (e.g., targeted advertising) under applicable state law. You can opt out of such tracking by clicking the “Your Privacy Choices” link in the footer of our website, which will allow you to access our cookie and tracking preference center and select or modify your preferences. Please note that this mechanism provides opt-out rights under applicable state privacy laws. You may also opt out by enabling a recognized opt-out preference signal, such as the Global Privacy Control (“GPC”), on your browser or device. See “Making Requests to Opt-Out; Opt-Out Preference Signals and Universal Opt-Out Mechanisms” below for additional details.
The purpose of this Privacy Policy is to provide you with a comprehensive description of our online and offline practices regarding our Information Practices. You have certain rights to control the use of your personal information. This privacy policy describes your rights as it relates to our Information Practices as to your personal information and how you can exercise those rights.

Unless otherwise allowed by applicable state law, we will only collect certain types of sensitive personal information with your consent. We will only collect, process, or disclose sensitive personal data where it is strictly necessary to provide or maintain a specific product or service you have requested, and we will not sell sensitive personal data. Mobile information. We do not share mobile information with third parties or affiliates for marketing or promotional purposes. Text messaging originator opt-in data and consent are excluded from all categories of information we disclose and will not be shared with any third parties, excluding aggregators and providers of the Text Message services.
De-identified and aggregated information. We may disclose aggregated information about our users and de-identified data or information that does not identify an individual, without restriction.
Where we maintain de-identified data (data that cannot reasonably be used to infer information about, or otherwise be linked to, an identified or identifiable individual), we commit to the following: (1) we take reasonable measures to ensure the data cannot be associated with an individual; (2) we publicly commit to maintaining and using the data in de-identified form and will not attempt to re-identify the data, except for the purpose of determining whether our de-identification processes comply with applicable law; and (3) we contractually obligate any recipients of the de-identified data to comply with all applicable requirements regarding de-identified data. Data used for usage analytics and business intelligence may be anonymized or aggregated so that resulting insights do not constitute Personal Information.

Right to Opt-Out of Sale/Sharing; Targeted Advertising: You may have the right to “opt out” of the “sale” or “sharing” of your personal information for cross-context behavioral advertising, or processing of your personal data for purposes of targeted advertising (as those terms are defined by applicable state law).
Right to Withdraw Consent: Where we have collected or used your personal information based on your consent, you have the right to withdraw your consent at any time.
Right to Appeal: If we deny your request to exercise any of the rights described above, you may have the right to appeal that decision by submitting a request to appeal to us.
Right to Non-Discrimination: We will not discriminate against you for exercising any of your state privacy rights. Making Requests to Know/Access, Delete, or Correct: You may exercise your rights, as applicable by state law, by submitting a verified consumer request to know information (and to receive your personal information in a portable format), to correct information, or for deletion.

Please note that, depending on the applicable state law, we may not be required to provide personal information to you if you make multiple requests in a certain time period.

We will verify your request using the information you provide through the request form or in your communication. If you submit a request that is not through one of the above designated methods or is deficient in some manner unrelated to verification, we will provide you with information on how to submit the request or remedy any deficiencies with your request. We cannot respond to your request or provide you with personal information if we cannot verify your identity or authority to make the request. We will confirm receipt of your request and respond, consistent with applicable law, if we can verify your identity. Requests for deletion will require separate confirmation that you want your information deleted. We do not knowingly collect information of minors under the age of 18.

We recognize and process opt-out preference signals and UOOMs as valid opt-out requests as required by applicable state law. The game of the year is finally here
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
02:53:15 UTC
11:53:15 KST

Hi, friends! Welcome to Installer No. 153, your guide to the best and Verge-iest stuff in the world. (If you’re new here, welcome, thank you for tearing yourself away from GTA to be here, and also you can read all the old editions at the Installer homepage.)
The game of the year is finally here
Plus, in this week’s Installer: A better YouTube Kids, new ways to get stuff done, the FTX story on Netflix, and more.
This week, I’ve been reading about nuclear radiation and F1 brains and phone scammers, watching about 10 minutes of Madden before bailing, playing a lot of Madden NFL instead, learning my way around Instagram Edits on the Mac, trying the 12-3-30 workout while watching Mission: Impossible movies in half-hour increments, getting my new Apple HomeView up and running (still don’t know why I bought one…), and dreaming up stuff to do with the Flipper One.
I also have for you the game of the year (decade? century?), a couple of great new productivity tools, a Netflix take on the FTX story, and much more. But mostly, it’s GTA week. Let’s do it.
Also, programming note: Installer is off next week. It’s Thanksgiving, there’s food to eat and football to watch. Hope you have a great couple of weeks, and we’ll be so back in December.
(As always, the best part of Installer is your ideas and tips. What have you been watching / reading / listening to / playing / adding to your holiday lists this week? Tell me everything: installer@theverge.com. And if you know someone else who might enjoy Installer, forward it to them and tell them to subscribe here.)
The Drop
- Grand Theft Auto VI. I mean, this literally could be the whole newsletter. But it appears this game is everything we’ve been waiting for, and I anticipate spending too much of the rest of my life playing it. No regrets.
- The GTA VI soundtrack. I can’t think of many games whose soundtracks have this kind of power: It’s a 34-song compilation, including a bunch of new music from huge artists. I’m going to listen to this while I work, which I assume will make all my writing cooler and more crime-filled.
- The Teenage Engineering EP/BC-1. I’ve been thinking about rebooting my physical media collection, and I can’t think of a better way to start than with this beautiful CD player. Like all Teenage Engineering gear, it is somehow retro and futuristic at the same time, and it’ll slide neatly into a synth setup or just look extremely rad in your living room.
- Alfred 6. A long-awaited update to the launcher and workflow tool so many Mac users love. I see some nice interface changes here but otherwise not a massively different product… but that’s actually probably the right call. Alfred is very good at being Alfred.
- The TRMNL Frame. A 31.5-inch, programmable color E Ink screen that you can hang on your wall. This either immediately excites you, and $1,299 is a small price to pay, or does nothing for you and is way too expensive. I accept both reactions equally. I also want one so badly.
- Tasks in Gemini. A stupid, stupid, stupid name for an otherwise useful product! Gemini now has a lot of the same “do stuff for you” tools that Muse and Dots offer — this time with much better access to your Google data. I’m slowly working through my inbox with this one.
- Who Wants This? Fun new podcast in which two writers and comedians attempt to make sense of current trends, and whether anyone actually… wants this. The first episode is about protein and proteinmaxxing, and I both enjoyed the hang and learned a lot. It also made me feel like everyone has just completely lost their minds.
- The Altruists. We are due for many, many fictionalized versions of the FTX story. I’ve heard and read some mixed things about this new Netflix series, but I dig the idea of trying to play it as both a love story and a scam story. It really is both those things!
- ClockwiseTV. Respect to Hank Green for being one of the few people bold enough to try and compete with YouTube. The pitch here is smart and simple: curated content for kids, on a timer, no ads, no autoplay. This is immediately going on all my iPads.
- “CIA Meeting Cold Open.” Last week’s SNL was really good overall (though maybe I just love John Mulaney?) but the cold open, with the tech billionaires learning how not to fall for a honeypot, absolutely slayed me. The spray bottle on Elon Musk makes me laugh every single time.
You may not know Tom Hovey, but I can practically guarantee you’ve seen his work. That’s because, in addition to many other cool projects, Tom’s also the artist behind those illustrated graphics of everyone’s bakes on The Great British Baking Show. His unique style has been all over that show, and my TV screen, for many years.
I found Tom’s work recently on Threads, when he went massively viral after debunking the idea that all those food drawings are AI with a video to prove how he does it. He’s been working with digital tools for a decade, he said, “but never ever AI.”
I asked Tom to share his homescreen with us to see how he might use his phone for his work, and also to see if I might learn any good GBBS spoilers. (Alas, none.) Here it is, plus some info on the apps he uses and why:
The phone: iPhone 17 Pro.
The wallpaper: A sunrise photo of the Swan River, where I run most mornings in Perth Western Australia. We moved here permanently 6 months ago.
The apps: Apple Podcasts, Brick, Sonos, Strava, SoundCloud, Photos, Google, Google Maps, iNaturalist, Google Calendar, Chrome, NYT Games, Gmail, WhatsApp, Spotify, Discord.
Like many people I’m addicted to my phone, so bricking my phone when I get home from the studio has been amazing. I’m obsessed with running, so Strava and Runna are used every day for training and logging my runs but also hyping up my running pals.
Six apps for my kids’ primary school is too many apps.
I have weather apps but it’s kinda unnecessary now we live in Australia, because it’s either hot or really hot!
iNaturalist is my newest app and I love it. It’s for documenting nature — flora and fauna. As I’m in a new country, I’m enjoying discovering all the native flowers and plants I find on runs. I’ve also recently done some trial runs in Perth Hills, where I’ve run into tons of wild kangaroos and snakes. So fun! My kids also love bugs and birds, so learning all about them is cool. Also when you add your photos of nature, it geotags them and sends the details to some scientists who can understand the areas you are documenting.
I also asked Tom to share a few things he’s into right now. Here’s what he sent back:
- I listen to a lot of audiobooks while I’m at my desk drawing and also while running, and audiobooks are the only way I can “read” non-fiction as I just fall asleep immediately if I try and actually read them. Some recent audiobooks I’ve enjoyed: The Castle by Jon Ronson; Thirst: Twelve Drinks That Changed My Life by John Robins; Hidden Valley Road by Robert Kolker; London Falling by Patrick Radden Keefe.
- TV shows I’ve recently watched: The Pitt, Widow’s Bay, This Is a Gardening Show, East of Eden, Star City, Pluribus.
- As an illustrator I’m always looking for excellent people making cool things and in a world where AI art is taking over I am seeking more and more analog art so here are some of the coolest creatives I’m currently enjoying: Natalie Andrewson, Sean Morris, Carla Adams, Ellis Parrinder, Azpeger Tat, and Kawafi.
- I have a WhatsApp chat with a bunch of my old illustrator pals which is a bunch of old men gossiping and sending each other Paul McCartney memes.
- Really enjoying the internet’s response to Damian Lewis’s foray into music.
Crowdsourced
Here’s what the Installer community is into this week. I want to know what you’re into right now as well! Email installer@theverge.com or message me on Signal — @davidpierce.11 — with your recommendations for anything and everything, and we’ll feature some of our favorites here every week. For even more great recommendations, check out the replies to this post on Threads and this post on Bluesky.

It is a well-written investigation into the effects of cancel culture on public debate and education. Wherever you fall on the political spectrum, this book will challenge you to think about how we should engage with ‘dangerous’ and ‘offensive’ ideas.” — Josh
“Been watching From since the first three seasons are available on Netflix right now. It’s a good show, has very Lost vibes to it.” — Bobby
Signing off
One of the most underrated strange things about the tech industry is how much The Social Network changed the way people think about Mark Zuckerberg. Yes, the movie takes huge liberties, and yes, every single person involved with the movie was quick to remind us that it was fiction. But for so many people, Jesse Eisenberg’s character just became who Zuck was.
I think Andrew Garfield in Artificial might be about to do the same thing to Sam Altman. The new trailer looks fabulous, all the early buzz about the movie seems to be great, and the movie absolutely could not be more perfectly timed. It’s apparently a pretty brutal look at Altman, OpenAI, and the whole AI movement — I wonder how Garfield and his handsomeness might bend the way the world responds. I cannot wait to see this movie.
See you in two weeks!

Clojure will affect the way you think about programming (2017)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-21
02:40:39 UTC
11:40:39 KST

I sometimes get asked for recommendations of what programming language to "learn next". It's great that folks appreciate the fact that being exposed to several sufficiently different programming languages is an important step on the path to programming enlightenment. But it's also true that, given limited free time, the choice of which languages to learn is important. As the famous quote by Alan Perlis says:
A language that doesn't affect the way you think about programming, is not
worth knowing.
In this post I want to explain why I think Clojure is a terrific language for this task. Whatever your background, it's almost guaranteed to affect the way you think about programming. It's a fairly new language (from 2007) that did a good job of collecting insights and paradigms from many existing languages and organizing them into a coherent whole. It's chock-full of great ideas. In other words, the perfect language to expand your brain.
It's a Lisp!
First and foremost, Clojure belongs to the Lisp family of languages, like Common Lisp, Scheme and Racket. Lisp is one of the oldest and still most important families of programming languages to be familiar with. Being a Lisp in this case means several things:
- Clojure is a dynamic language. No matter where you stand on the static vs. dynamic typing debate, knowing languages in both camps is important. Clojure has a kind of optional typing, but in essence it's dynamic.
- It promotes combinations of built-in data structures (lists, maps, vectors) over objects and has very good reasons for doing so.
- It promotes programming with higher-order functions. Some built-in features like reducers and transducers rely heavily on composing higher order functions for transforming other functions.
- It has uniform syntax with full Lisp-style macro support! This capability has proved to be very elusive for non-Lisp languages, and seeing some real macros in action is enlightening. Clojure has a whole Go-like CSP equivalent implemented using macros in core.async.
- It encourages REPL-based development, where functionality is built and testsed gradually from the bottom up in an interactive terminal.
Importantly, Clojure is a very modern Lisp, with tons of libraries for tasks common in our day and age. It also builds upon important recent research in data structures, such as efficient persistent vectors, that only appeared in the 1990s or later. I've been dabbling with various Lisps for almost 15 years, and Clojure is the first Lisp I'd consider using in production.
I want the stress the importance of full Lisp-style macros once again. Lisp's (and Clojure's) uniform syntax and macros force us to think about the Meta-circular abstraction - there's just no way around it! We have to think of our code as data, and this has profound implications. Back in 2005, when I was still a programming neophyte, I faced a performance challenge for a data-decoding program I was hacking on in Perl. Having learned some Lisp, I had the insight of generating and evaluating new Perl code on the fly, which turned out to be key to the success of that program. I'm confident I could not have come up with that solution back then without being aware of the "Lisp way".
Pragmatism and hard-won experience
The designers of Clojure are extremely pragmatic, building upon decades of industry experience. I strongly recommend watching some YouTube videos of talks by leading Clojure developers. In particular, watch a few of the more popular talks by Rich Hickey - Clojure's original designer. The Alan Perlis quote mentioned above may be his most popular, but there's another I find at least as insightful:
Simplicity does not precede complexity, but follows it.
There's a certain quality in programmers I believe we can all recognize. It can only come from real, hard-won experience of building systems for many years and pondering about how to build such systems more effectively next time. Rich Hickey certainly belongs to this quality category - his talks are very insightful, and this philosophy reigns in the design and implementation of Clojure, as well as its (friendly and vibrant) community.
Sequences and laziness for powerful in-language data processing
Applications that process and extract useful bits of information from large datasets all look alike in many programming languages, at least to some extent. What we really want in many cases is SQL-like primitives built into our languages, but this is often challenging (.NET's LINQ is one example of a successful approach).
Clojure combines pervasive sequence protocols with persistent data structures and laziness to make this kind of task natural, using only built-in tools. Here's a realistic example of a function taken from the Clojure Applied book:
(defn revenue-by-department [carts]
  (->> (filter :settled? carts)
       (mapcat :line-items)
       (map line-summary)
       (group-by :dept)
       (reduce-kv dept-total)))
All the functions used here are built-ins, including the ->> macro. This code reads like SQL and it's efficient too. In fact, it can be made somewhat more efficient and even seamlessly parallelized using Reducers and Transducers.
The clojure.spec library provides a degree of type-safety for nested data structures. If you've ever written comments like "this maps strings to lists, where each list element is a map of ...", clojure.spec makes it more formal and verifiable. This is very useful when working with data, and is another example of where Clojure's pragmatism shines; the language is dynamic in its core, but when you need static checking - it's there, optionally (core.typed is another option for a more statically typed flavor).
The right approach to object-orientation
Historically, Lisp programmers weren't the biggest proponents of OOP. This doesn't mean that OOP has absolutely no value in Lisp-y languages, however. Common Lisp has had CLOS for decades, and Clojure also comes with an array of OO-like features.
That said, Clojure's flavor of OOP is particularly powerful and tends to discourage bad practices. Clojure uses "protocols", which are a kind of interfaces, and encourages thinking in terms of protocols rather than in terms of classes with inheritance hierarchies, sort of like Go. Add to that an ability to do true multiple dispatch and you have a powerful modeling tool at your fingertips.
Built-in support for concurrency and parallelism
It wouldn't be a real language of the 2000s without powerful support for concurrency and parallelism right at the core. Clojure supports this in many ways, which are interleaved.
First, it's a functional language with persistent data structures. Persistent data structures are effectively immutable, which makes them very attractive in a multi-threaded context. Clojure has a great implementation of persistent data structures even for things like vectors (which are quite challenging to implement in an efficient manner). This is a bigger deal than may originally appear. Pure functions are often great in theory but fail in practice due to too much copying of non-trivial data structures; Clojure's elegant usage of persistent data structures solves this problem, making pure functions efficient and thus much more applicable to a wide array of problems.
Second, Clojure doesn't lock itself in the closet of purism and does support lower-level primitives for concurrency and mutation, where needed. Atoms are built-in mutable state units with atomic updates. Refs and transactions go further, implementing STM.
Third, Clojure comes with a bunch of concurrency tools built in. Promises, futures and threads leverage the underlying JVM's threading utilities.
Finally, Clojure has a pretty good implementation of Go's CSP in the core.async module. It provides two useful things:
- Channels and Go select-like functionality (alts!!) which makes it easy to work with safe concurrent queues and "share data by communicating".
- go-blocks with non-blocking primitives that make it possible to write cooperative "green threads" with ease.
Conclusion - try it
Finally, it's important to mention that Clojure is far from being an academic exercise. It's used in production by several companies, and has strong roots in the massive Java ecosystem. Clojure's interoperability with Java is seamless - Java code can be called from Clojure with no special provisions, and all core Clojure entities are Java objects deep under the hood.
On the client side, Clojurescript has been gaining traction recently as another language-compiled-to-JS option. It brings the elegance of Clojure to client-side programming, among with some unique features like core.async for callback-free concurrency.
This doesn't mean that you should only consider Clojure as a fit for your next project. On the contrary, I recommend learning if even if there's little chance of using it in production any time soon. In fact, this was exactly my situation, Clojure is a great language to expand your programming horizons; who knows, maybe in the future you'll find it useful for some real work. If you do, great! If you don't, learning it will positively affect how you use other programming languages.

====================================================================================================

worth knowing.
In this post I want to explain why I think Clojure is a terrific language for this task. Whatever your background, it's almost guaranteed to affect the way you think about programming. It's a fairly new language (from 2007) that did a good job of collecting insights and paradigms from many existing languages and organizing them into a coherent whole. It's chock-full of great ideas. In other words, the perfect language to expand your brain.
It's a Lisp!
First and foremost, Clojure belongs to the Lisp family of languages, like Common Lisp, Scheme and Racket. Lisp is one of the oldest and still most important families of programming languages to be familiar with. Being a Lisp in this case means several things:
- Clojure is a dynamic language. No matter where you stand on the static vs. dynamic typing debate, knowing languages in both camps is important. Clojure has a kind of optional typing, but in essence it's dynamic.
- It promotes combinations of built-in data structures (lists, maps, vectors) over objects and has very good reasons for doing so.
- It promotes programming with higher-order functions. Some built-in features like reducers and transducers rely heavily on composing higher order functions for transforming other functions.
- It has uniform syntax with full Lisp-style macro support! This capability has proved to be very elusive for non-Lisp languages, and seeing some real macros in action is enlightening. Clojure has a whole Go-like CSP equivalent implemented using macros in core.async.
- It encourages REPL-based development, where functionality is built and testsed gradually from the bottom up in an interactive terminal.
Importantly, Clojure is a very modern Lisp, with tons of libraries for tasks common in our day and age. It also builds upon important recent research in data structures, such as efficient persistent vectors, that only appeared in the 1990s or later. I've been dabbling with various Lisps for almost 15 years, and Clojure is the first Lisp I'd consider using in production.
I want the stress the importance of full Lisp-style macros once again. Lisp's (and Clojure's) uniform syntax and macros force us to think about the Meta-circular abstraction - there's just no way around it! We have to think of our code as data, and this has profound implications. Back in 2005, when I was still a programming neophyte, I faced a performance challenge for a data-decoding program I was hacking on in Perl. Having learned some Lisp, I had the insight of generating and evaluating new Perl code on the fly, which turned out to be key to the success of that program. I'm confident I could not have come up with that solution back then without being aware of the "Lisp way".
Pragmatism and hard-won experience
The designers of Clojure are extremely pragmatic, building upon decades of industry experience. I strongly recommend watching some YouTube videos of talks by leading Clojure developers. In particular, watch a few of the more popular talks by Rich Hickey - Clojure's original designer. The Alan Perlis quote mentioned above may be his most popular, but there's another I find at least as insightful:
Simplicity does not precede complexity, but follows it.
There's a certain quality in programmers I believe we can all recognize. It can only come from real, hard-won experience of building systems for many years and pondering about how to build such systems more effectively next time. Rich Hickey certainly belongs to this quality category - his talks are very insightful, and this philosophy reigns in the design and implementation of Clojure, as well as its (friendly and vibrant) community.
Sequences and laziness for powerful in-language data processing
Applications that process and extract useful bits of information from large datasets all look alike in many programming languages, at least to some extent. What we really want in many cases is SQL-like primitives built into our languages, but this is often challenging (.NET's LINQ is one example of a successful approach).
Clojure combines pervasive sequence protocols with persistent data structures and laziness to make this kind of task natural, using only built-in tools. Here's a realistic example of a function taken from the Clojure Applied book:
(defn revenue-by-department [carts]
  (->> (filter :settled? carts)
       (mapcat :line-items)
       (map line-summary)
       (group-by :dept)
       (reduce-kv dept-total)))
All the functions used here are built-ins, including the ->> macro. This code reads like SQL and it's efficient too. In fact, it can be made somewhat more efficient and even seamlessly parallelized using Reducers and Transducers.
The clojure.spec library provides a degree of type-safety for nested data structures. If you've ever written comments like "this maps strings to lists, where each list element is a map of ...", clojure.spec makes it more formal and verifiable. This is very useful when working with data, and is another example of where Clojure's pragmatism shines; the language is dynamic in its core, but when you need static checking - it's there, optionally (core.typed is another option for a more statically typed flavor).
The right approach to object-orientation
Historically, Lisp programmers weren't the biggest proponents of OOP. This doesn't mean that OOP has absolutely no value in Lisp-y languages, however. Common Lisp has had CLOS for decades, and Clojure also comes with an array of OO-like features.
That said, Clojure's flavor of OOP is particularly powerful and tends to discourage bad practices. Clojure uses "protocols", which are a kind of interfaces, and encourages thinking in terms of protocols rather than in terms of classes with inheritance hierarchies, sort of like Go. Add to that an ability to do true multiple dispatch and you have a powerful modeling tool at your fingertips.
Built-in support for concurrency and parallelism
It wouldn't be a real language of the 2000s without powerful support for concurrency and parallelism right at the core. Clojure supports this in many ways, which are interleaved.
First, it's a functional language with persistent data structures. Persistent data structures are effectively immutable, which makes them very attractive in a multi-threaded context. Clojure has a great implementation of persistent data structures even for things like vectors (which are quite challenging to implement in an efficient manner). This is a bigger deal than may originally appear. Pure functions are often great in theory but fail in practice due to too much copying of non-trivial data structures; Clojure's elegant usage of persistent data structures solves this problem, making pure functions efficient and thus much more applicable to a wide array of problems.
Second, Clojure doesn't lock itself in the closet of purism and does support lower-level primitives for concurrency and mutation, where needed. Atoms are built-in mutable state units with atomic updates. Refs and transactions go further, implementing STM.
Third, Clojure comes with a bunch of concurrency tools built in. Promises, futures and threads leverage the underlying JVM's threading utilities.
Finally, Clojure has a pretty good implementation of Go's CSP in the core.async module. It provides two useful things:
- Channels and Go select-like functionality (alts!!) which makes it easy to work with safe concurrent queues and "share data by communicating".
- go-blocks with non-blocking primitives that make it possible to write cooperative "green threads" with ease.
Conclusion - try it
Finally, it's important to mention that Clojure is far from being an academic exercise. It's used in production by several companies, and has strong roots in the massive Java ecosystem. Clojure's interoperability with Java is seamless - Java code can be called from Clojure with no special provisions, and all core Clojure entities are Java objects deep under the hood.
On the client side, Clojurescript has been gaining traction recently as another language-compiled-to-JS option. It brings the elegance of Clojure to client-side programming, among with some unique features like core.async for callback-free concurrency.
This doesn't mean that you should only consider Clojure as a fit for your next project. On the contrary, I recommend learning if even if there's little chance of using it in production any time soon. In fact, this was exactly my situation, Clojure is a great language to expand your programming horizons; who knows, maybe in the future you'll find it useful for some real work. If you do, great! If you don't, learning it will positively affect how you use other programming languages.
